The function \(\mathcal W_m\) is nondecreasing on \((-\infty,0)\) and
nonincreasing on \((0,\infty)\).  For every real \(x\),
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal W_m(x)\longrightarrow\Up(x).                        \label{eq:four-step-limit}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

The proof of pointwise convergence passes through a useful exact comparison.  Set
\(h_m=2^{-m-1}\), and use the finite atomic law \(\nu_m\) from
\eqref{eq:finite-atomic-law}.  Whenever \(a\le b\),
\begin{equation}
 \nu_m\bigl((a+h_m,b-h_m]\bigr)
 \ \le\ \int_a^b\mathcal W_m(x)\dd x
 \ \le\ \nu_m\bigl((a-h_m,b+h_m]\bigr).                       \label{eq:four-step-sandwich}
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
 \lim_{m\to\infty}\int_a^b\mathcal W_m(x)\dd x
 =\int_a^b\Up(x)\dd x.                                       \label{eq:four-step-interval-limit}
\end{equation}
Monotonicity on the two open half-lines, the exact value at the origin, and continuity of
\(\Up\) then upgrade these interval limits to \eqref{eq:four-step-limit}.

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The polynomial recursion, product, degree, total coefficient mass, and bounded-partition
interpretation are respectively proved by
\lean{approximationPolynomial_succ},
the definition \lean{approximationPolynomial},
\lean{approximationPolynomial_natDegree},
\lean{approximationDegree_eq_sub},
\lean{approximationPolynomial_eval_one}, and
\lean{approximationPolynomial_coeff_eq_card} in
\path{EarlyApproximants.lean}.  The finite atomic description is identified with
\lean{finiteConvolutionMeasure} by
\lean{polynomialMeasure_eq_finiteConvolutionMeasure} in
\path{EarlyMeasureBridge.lean}.

For nonnegativity, see \lean{stepApproximant_nonneg} in
\path{EarlyApproximants.lean}.  The remaining analytic assertions are
\lean{stepApproximant_le_one},
\lean{stepApproximant_monotoneOn_Iio},
\lean{stepApproximant_antitoneOn_Ioi},
\lean{stepApproximant_apply_zero}, and
\lean{stepApproximant_tendsto_fabius} in
\path{StepApproximationLimit.lean}.  The normalization, the two sides of
\eqref{eq:four-step-sandwich}, and \eqref{eq:four-step-interval-limit} are
\lean{integral_stepApproximant},
\lean{finiteConvolutionMeasure_inner_le_intervalIntegral_stepApproximant},
\lean{intervalIntegral_stepApproximant_le_finiteConvolutionMeasure_outer}, and
\lean{intervalIntegral_stepApproximant_tendsto_of_le} in
\path{StepMeasureBridge.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{warning}[Why the endpoint convention is explicit]
The half-weighted cell representative agrees almost everywhere with the ordinary interval
indicator, so it has the same integral.  Its point values are nevertheless part of the
formal statement: adjacent cells share endpoints, and each contributes exactly half of its
coefficient there.  In particular, the corrected representative satisfies
\(\mathcal W_m(0)=1\) at every level.  These facts are formalized by
\lean{halfEndpointIntervalIndicator_ae_eq_indicator},
\lean{intervalIntegral_halfEndpointIntervalIndicator_of_le}, and
\lean{stepApproximant_apply_zero}.
\end{warning}

\subsection{Centered finite splines and an explicit error bound}
\label{sec:centered-splines}

The preceding finite random sums also give a completely explicit polynomial approximation
to $F$.  Put
\begin{equation}
 T_p=\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}\frac{U_i}{2^{i+1}},
 \qquad
 C_p(x)=\Prob\left(T_p\le x-2^{-p-1}\right),               \label{eq:centered-partial-cdf}
\end{equation}
where $p\ge1$.  The shift by half of the omitted tail is important: it centers the
one-sided approximation and improves its error by a factor of two.

Let
\begin{equation}
 N_p(x)=\max\left(0,\left\lfloor 2^p x+\frac12\right\rfloor\right),
 \qquad \varepsilon_r=(-1)^{\wt(r)},                       \label{eq:centered-cutoff}
\end{equation}
and interpret a sum with $N_p(x)=0$ as empty.  Define
\begin{equation}
 S_p(x)=
 \frac{(-1)^p}{2^{\binom p2}p!}
 \sum_{r=0}^{N_p(x)-1}
 \varepsilon_r
 \left(r-2^p x+\frac12\right)^p.                          \label{eq:centered-spline}
\end{equation}
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
 S_p(x)=
 \frac{1}{2^{\binom p2}p!}
 \sum_{r\ge0}\varepsilon_r
 \left(2^p x-\frac12-r\right)_+^p,                        \label{eq:centered-spline-positive}
\end{equation}
where only the terms $r<N_p(x)$ can be nonzero and $t_+=\max(t,0)$.
The cutoff in \eqref{eq:centered-cutoff} is a safe natural-number form of the
inclusive upper limit
\[
 0\le r\le\left\lfloor2^p x-\frac12\right\rfloor.
\]
It handles the empty case without assigning a negative upper endpoint to a finite sum.

\begin{theorem}[Centered spline as a finite distribution function]
\label{thm:centered-spline-cdf}
For $p\ge1$ and $0\le x\le1$,
\begin{equation}
 S_p(x)=C_p(x).                                            \label{eq:spline-equals-cdf}
\end{equation}
Consequently $0\le S_p(x)\le1$ on the unit interval.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $p=1$, $T_1=U_0/2$, and direct evaluation gives
\[
 C_1(x)=\max\left(0,\min\left(1,2x-\frac12\right)\right).
\]
Formula \eqref{eq:centered-spline-positive} gives exactly the same broken-linear
function: the term $r=0$ starts at $x=1/4$, and the term $r=1$ subtracts the excess
after $x=3/4$.

For the induction step, split off the first uniform coordinate.  The finite sums satisfy
\[
 T_{p+1}\ \overset{\mathrm d}=\ \frac{U_0+T_p'}2,
\]
where $T_p'$ is an independent copy of $T_p$.  Conditioning on $U_0$ and accounting for
the midpoint shifts gives the exact smoothing recurrence
\begin{equation}
 C_{p+1}(x)=\int_0^1 C_p(2x-u)\dd u.                      \label{eq:centered-cdf-smoothing}
\end{equation}
On the spline side, integrate each truncated power in
\eqref{eq:centered-spline-positive}.  The two binary children $2r$ and $2r+1$
have opposite Thue--Morse signs, because
\(\varepsilon_{2r}=\varepsilon_r\) and
\(\varepsilon_{2r+1}=-\varepsilon_r\).  After the change of scale this yields
\begin{equation}
 S_{p+1}(x)=\int_0^1 S_p(2x-u)\dd u.                      \label{eq:centered-spline-smoothing}
\end{equation}
The normalization is exactly the one required by
\[
 \sum_{r=0}^{2^p-1}\varepsilon_r r^p
 =(-1)^p2^{\binom p2}p!,
\]
while every lower power sum vanishes.  Thus the two recurrences and their common base
case prove \eqref{eq:spline-equals-cdf}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Quantitative centered approximation]
\label{thm:centered-spline-error}
For $p\ge1$ and $0\le x\le1$,
\begin{equation}
 F(x-2^{-p-1})\le S_p(x)\le F(x+2^{-p-1}),                \label{eq:centered-sandwich}
\end{equation}
and hence
\begin{equation}
 |S_p(x)-F(x)|\le2^{-p}.                                  \label{eq:centered-error}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $X=T_p+R_p$, where the omitted tail satisfies
\[
 0\le R_p=\sum_{i=p}^{\infty}\frac{U_i}{2^{i+1}}\le2^{-p}.
\]
If $X\le x-2^{-p-1}$, then certainly
$T_p\le x-2^{-p-1}$.  Conversely, if
$T_p\le x-2^{-p-1}$, then
$X\le x+2^{-p-1}$.  Taking probabilities and using
\eqref{eq:F-CDF} and \eqref{eq:spline-equals-cdf} proves
\eqref{eq:centered-sandwich}.

The bounded Fabius function is globally $2$-Lipschitz.  Indeed, on the open unit interval
its derivative is a rescaled value of $\Up$ (or of the signed extension), whose absolute
value is at most $1$, and outside the unit interval $F$ is constant.  Thus
\[
 |F(x\pm2^{-p-1})-F(x)|\le2\cdot2^{-p-1}=2^{-p}.
\]
Combining these two inequalities with the sandwich gives
\eqref{eq:centered-error}.
\end{proof}

The bound is uniform on the closed interval and includes both endpoints.  It is stronger
than mere weak convergence of the finite distributions, and it is the quantitative input
for the computability proof in \cref{sec:effective-computability}.

The same finite formula also has the correct signed global behavior.  For $x\le0$ its safe
cutoff is empty, so $S_p(x)=\mathcal F(x)=0$.  On the nonnegative half-line, binary block
translation and midpoint reflection extend the local identification.  Consequently, for
every $p\ge1$ and every $x\in\R$,
\begin{equation}
 |S_p(x)-\mathcal F(x)|\le2^{-p}.                         \label{eq:centered-spline-global-limit}
\end{equation}
Indeed, every nonnegative two-unit block is a common signed copy of the first block, and
on the second unit cell both functions use the same complement.  Neither operation changes
the absolute error in \eqref{eq:centered-error}; the nonpositive half-line is exact.  This
pointwise all-real bound is
\lean{abs_fabiusUniformSpline_sub_extendedFabius_le}; it implies the separately proved
uniform convergence \lean{fabiusUniformSpline_tendstoUniformly_extendedFabius}.  This
global form strengthens and packages the analytic input used by the complex-shift and
discrete-limit results below.

\section{Fourier products, inversion, and Poisson summation}
\label{sec:fourier}

Use the convention
\begin{equation}
 \widehat f(z)=\int_\R f(x)\e^{-2\pi\ii xz}\dd x.          \label{eq:fourier-convention}
\end{equation}
The formal Fourier transform of \(\Up\) is entire and has two exact infinite-product
descriptions.

\begin{theorem}[Sinc and cosine products]
\label{thm:four-products}
For every \(z\in\C\),
\begin{align}
 \widehat{\Up}(z)
 &=\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}
   \sinc\!\left(\frac{\pi z}{2^n}\right),                 \label{eq:sinc-product}\\
 &=\prod_{m=1}^{\infty}
   \left(\cos\frac{\pi z}{2^m}\right)^m.                  \label{eq:four-product-cos}
\end{align}
The transform also satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \widehat{\Up}(z)=\sinc(\pi z)\widehat{\Up}(z/2).          \label{eq:fourier-refinement}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The finite-product refinement and its infinite limit are
\lean{rvachevFourier_eq_finite_product} and
\lean{rvachevFourier_eq_product} in \path{FourierProduct.lean}.
The refinement equation is \lean{rvachevFourier_scaling} in the same module.
Writing \(\Phi\) for the first product in \eqref{eq:sinc-product}, the
standalone one-step and \(n\)-fold
renormalizations
\[
 \Phi(z)=\sinc(\pi z)\Phi(z/2),\qquad
 \Phi(z)=\left(\prod_{j<n}\sinc(\pi z/2^j)\right)\Phi(z/2^n)
\]
are \lean{rvachevFourierProduct_scaling} and
\lean{rvachevFourierProduct_shell} in
\path{RenormalizationIdentity.lean}.
The whole-plane analyticity theorem is
\lean{rvachevFourier_analyticOnNhd} in
\path{FourierAnalytic.lean}.  The weighted cosine product is
\lean{rvachevFourierProduct_eq_cosineProduct} in
\path{OriginalPaperSupplement.lean}; its finite telescoping identity is proved internally in
the same module.
\end{proof}

\subsection{A finite Thue--Morse product-to-sum bridge}

The finite binary block has a reflection law that matches the parity of a
quarter-turn phase.  Recall that
\(\varepsilon_n=(-1)^{\wt(n)}\).

\begin{proposition}[Binary complement and quarter-turn parity]
\label{prop:binary-complement-quarter-turn}
For \(m,n\in\N\) with \(n<2^m\),
\begin{align}
 \wt(2^m-1-n)+\wt(n)&=m,                                 \label{eq:binary-weight-complement}\\
 \varepsilon_{2^m-1-n}&=(-1)^m\varepsilon_n.             \label{eq:thue-sign-complement}
\end{align}
For every \(m\in\N\) and \(x\in\R\),
\begin{equation}
 (-1)^m\cos\!\left(\frac{m\pi}{2}-x\right)
 =\cos\!\left(\frac{m\pi}{2}+x\right).                 \label{eq:quarter-turn-phase-parity}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The three identities are respectively
\lean{binaryWeight_compl}, \lean{thueMorseSign_compl}, and
\lean{neg_one_pow_mul_cos_sub} in \path{LacunaryProductToSum.lean}.
The first hypothesis makes the natural-number subtractions in
\eqref{eq:binary-weight-complement}--\eqref{eq:thue-sign-complement}
ordinary binary complementation rather than truncated subtraction.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Affine Thue--Morse sine polynomial]
\label{thm:thue-morse-affine-sine}
For every \(m\in\N\) and \(x,\phi\in\R\),
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 \sum_{n=0}^{2^m-1}\varepsilon_n\sin(\phi+nx)
 =
 2^m\left(\prod_{j=0}^{m-1}\sin\frac{2^jx}{2}\right)
 \sin\!\left(
   \phi+\frac{(2^m-1)x}{2}-\frac{m\pi}{2}
 \right).}
 \label{eq:thue-morse-affine-sine}
\end{equation}
The product is empty when \(m=0\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
This is exactly \lean{sum_thueMorseSign_mul_sin_affine} in
\path{ThueMorseSineProduct.lean}.  Its proof rotates the complex
Thue--Morse exponential product through the arbitrary phase \(\phi\),
rewrites \((-2\ii)^m\) as a positive magnitude and a quarter-turn phase,
and takes imaginary parts.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Lacunary sine product as a Thue--Morse sum]
\label{thm:lacunary-sine-product-to-sum}
For every \(m\in\N\) and \(w\in\R\),
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 2^m\prod_{j=0}^{m}\sin(2^jw)
 =\sum_{n=0}^{2^m-1}\varepsilon_n
   \sin\!\left(\frac{m\pi}{2}+(2n+1)w\right).}
                                                               \label{eq:lacunary-sine-product-to-sum}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
This is exactly \lean{prod_sin_two_pow_eq_thueMorse_sum} in
\path{LacunaryProductToSum.lean}.  It is the specialization of
\eqref{eq:thue-morse-affine-sine} at
\[
 x=2w,\qquad \phi=\frac{m\pi}{2}+w.
\]
The final sine becomes \(\sin(2^mw)\), which appends the last factor to
the product over \(j<m\).  The formal statement is over real \(w\); no
complex continuation or neighboring cosine-product identity is asserted.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Dyadic shell factorization]
\label{thm:fourier-dyadic-shell}
For every \(k\in\N\) and \(z\in\C\), dilation by \(2^k\) extracts exactly the first
\(k\) factors of the sinc product:
\begin{equation}
 \widehat{\Up}(2^kz)
 =\left(\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\sinc\!\left(\pi 2^{j+1}z\right)\right)
   \widehat{\Up}(z).                                      \label{eq:fourier-dyadic-shell}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
This is \lean{rvachevFourier_two_pow_mul} in
\path{RvachevFourierDecay.lean}.  Its standalone-product form is
\lean{rvachevFourierProduct_two_pow_mul} in \path{SincProductShells.lean}.
On the real axis the removable singularity can be cleared without excluding the zero ray:
for every \(k\in\N\) and \(y\in\R\),
\begin{equation}
 \begin{split}
 &2^{k(k+1)/2}(\pi|y|)^k
   \left|\widehat{\Up}(2^ky)\right|\\
 &\qquad={}
 \left(\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\left|\sin(2^{j+1}\pi y)\right|\right)
   \left|\widehat{\Up}(y)\right|.
 \end{split}                                               \label{eq:fourier-dyadic-shell-cross}
\end{equation}
This total form includes \(y=0\), and at \(k=0\) both sides are the same Fourier modulus.
It is \lean{norm_rvachevFourier_two_pow_mul_cross} in
\path{RvachevFourierDecay.lean}; its product-level source is
\lean{norm_rvachevFourierProduct_two_pow_mul_cross} in
\path{SincProductShells.lean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Total arbitrary-prefix identity and envelope]
\label{thm:fourier-prefix-bound}
Write \(\Phi\) for the standalone sinc product in \eqref{eq:sinc-product}.
For every \(K\in\N\) and \(x\in\R\), clearing the first \(K\) sinc
denominators gives the total identity
\begin{equation}
 (\pi|x|)^K|\Phi(x)|
 =2^{\binom K2}
   \left(\prod_{j=0}^{K-1}
     \left|\sin\!\left(\frac{\pi x}{2^j}\right)\right|\right)
   \left|\Phi\!\left(\frac{x}{2^K}\right)\right|.       \label{eq:fourier-prefix-cross}
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}
 (\pi|x|)^K|\Phi(x)|\le 2^{\binom K2}.                  \label{eq:fourier-prefix-cross-bound}
\end{equation}
Both formulas include \(x=0\) and the empty prefix \(K=0\).  Only after
dividing do we require \(x\ne0\):
\begin{equation}
 |\Phi(x)|\le
 \frac{2^{\binom K2}}{(\pi|x|)^K}\qquad(x\ne0).       \label{eq:fourier-prefix-div-bound}
\end{equation}
Thus the usual selected-factor constant is
\(2^{K(K-1)/2}/\pi^K\), with no unnecessary assumption \(K\ge1\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The total identity, its total bound, and the divided corollary are respectively
\lean{norm_rvachevFourierProduct_prefix_cross},
\lean{norm_rvachevFourierProduct_prefix_cross_le}, and
\lean{norm_rvachevFourierProduct_le_prefix_div} in
\path{SincPrefixBound.lean}.  The proof combines the finite shell
\lean{rvachevFourierProduct_shell}, the removable real-sinc identity
\lean{abs_mul_norm_complexSinc_ofReal}, and the global tail bound
\lean{norm_rvachevFourierProduct_le_one}; no convergence argument is repeated.
\end{proof}

The mean-value input behind shell estimates is more general than the dyadic case.
Let \(m_0,m_1,\ldots\) be positive integer frequencies satisfying
\(2m_j\le m_{j+1}\).  Then, for every \(n\in\N\),
\begin{align}
 \int_0^1\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}\sin^2(\pi m_jt)\,\dd t
   &=\left(\frac12\right)^n,                              \label{eq:lacunary-square-mean}\\
 \int_0^1\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}|\sin(\pi m_jt)|\,\dd t
   &\le\left(\frac{\sqrt2}{2}\right)^n.                  \label{eq:lacunary-abs-mean}
\end{align}
The exact square mean is \lean{integral_prod_sin_sq} in
\path{LacunaryRieszIntegral.lean}.  Applying Cauchy--Schwarz to it gives
\lean{integral_prod_abs_sin_le_sqrt_two} in
\path{LacunaryMeanSharp.lean}.  The geometric-base and dyadic specializations are
\lean{integral_prod_abs_sin_pow_le_sqrt_two} and
\lean{integral_prod_abs_sin_two_pow_le_sqrt_two}.

The logarithm of the normalized dyadic sine shell has an exact square mean as well.  For
every \(n\in\N\), including the empty product \(n=0\),
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1
 \log^2\!\left|\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}2\sin(\pi2^jt)\right|\dd t
 =\frac{\pi^2}{4}n-\frac{\pi^2}{3}\left(1-2^{-n}\right).
                                                               \label{eq:dyadic-log-sine-variance}
\end{equation}
The product statement is \lean{integral_sq_log_prod_two_sin_all} in
\path{BirkhoffProductBridge.lean}.  Its doubling-cocycle form is
\lean{integral_sq_birkhoff_sum_all} in \path{BirkhoffVariance.lean}, and the finite
covariance summation is \lean{variance_closed_form_all} in
\path{VarianceBookkeeping.lean}.  The all-index declarations make the zero-length
boundary an identity rather than a side condition.

For a quantitative global form, set
\begin{equation}
 \kappa_\infty=\frac32+\frac{\log(\pi/\sqrt3)}{\log2},
 \qquad
 E_\kappa(x)=\exp\!\left(-\frac{(\log x)^2}{2\log2}\right)x^{-\kappa}
 \quad(x>0).                                               \label{eq:fourier-decay-gauge}
\end{equation}
There is a constant \(C>0\) such that the single two-sided estimate
\begin{equation}
 |\widehat{\Up}(x)|\le C E_{\kappa_\infty}(|x|)
 \qquad(|x|\ge1)                                          \label{eq:fourier-two-sided-envelope}
\end{equation}
holds on both real tails.  This is
\lean{norm_rvachevFourier_le_decayGauge_abs} in
\path{RvachevFourierDecay.lean}.  On the distinguished dyadic ray the same gauge occurs
in an exact identity:
\begin{equation}
 |\widehat{\Up}(2^k\,2/3)|
 =\frac{|\widehat{\Up}(2/3)|}{E_{\kappa_\infty}(2/3)}
   E_{\kappa_\infty}(2^k\,2/3),                           \label{eq:fourier-peak-ray-envelope}
\end{equation}
namely \lean{norm_rvachevFourier_peak_ray_envelope} in the same module.

The exponent \(\kappa_\infty\) is sharp within this family of gauges.  Write \(\Phi\) for
the standalone sinc product, which equals \(\widehat{\Up}\) by
\lean{rvachevFourier_eq_product}.  For every \(\kappa>\kappa_\infty\),
\begin{equation}
 \frac{|\Phi(2^k\,2/3)|}{E_\kappa(2^k\,2/3)}
 \longrightarrow+\infty
 \qquad(k\longrightarrow\infty).                            \label{eq:fourier-kappa-sharp}
\end{equation}
More explicitly, for every real \(C\) there is a \(k\in\N\) such that
\(x_k=2^k\,2/3\ge1\) and
\begin{equation}
 C E_\kappa(x_k)<|\Phi(x_k)|.                               \label{eq:fourier-kappa-obstruction}
\end{equation}
Consequently no \(C>0\) satisfies
\[
 |\Phi(x)|\le C E_\kappa(x)\qquad\text{for every real }x\ge1.
\]
The limit, explicit peak-ray obstruction, and positive-ray nonexistence theorem are
\lean{norm_rvachevFourierProduct_peak_ray_ratio_tendsto_atTop},
\lean{exists_peak_ray_norm_gt_mul_decayGauge}, and
\lean{not_exists_norm_rvachevFourierProduct_le_decayGauge_of_kappaInf_lt} in
\path{GlobalDecayEnvelope.lean}.

The transform also has the exact conjugation, parity, and real-axis identities
\begin{equation}
 \overline{\widehat{\Up}(z)}=\widehat{\Up}(-\overline z),
 \qquad \widehat{\Up}(-z)=\widehat{\Up}(z),
 \qquad \operatorname{Im}\widehat{\Up}(t)=0\quad(t\in\R).   \label{eq:fourier-symmetries}
\end{equation}
These are \lean{rvachevFourier_conj} in \path{FourierAnalytic.lean} and
\lean{rvachevFourier_neg}, \lean{rvachevFourier_ofReal_im_eq_zero} in
\path{FourierProduct.lean}.

\begin{theorem}[Exact Fourier zero set, positive-real zero-order limits, and canonical product]
\label{thm:fourier-zeros}
The complex zeros of the Fourier transform are precisely the nonzero integers:
\begin{equation}
 \widehat{\Up}(z)=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists\,m\in\Z\setminus\{0\}\text{ such that }z=m,
 \qquad z\in\C.                                           \label{eq:fourier-integer-zeros}
\end{equation}
At every positive integer \(m\), its real-axis zero order is exactly
\(\vtwo(m)+1\), in the following quantitative sense: there is a constant
\(C_m>0\) such that
\begin{equation}
 \lim_{\substack{x\to m\\x\ne m}}
 \frac{\left|\widehat{\Up}(x)\right|}
      {|x-m|^{\vtwo(m)+1}}
 =C_m.                                                     \label{eq:fourier-zero-order}
\end{equation}
The corresponding canonical product records all of these orders at once:
\begin{equation}
 \widehat{\Up}(z)=\prod_{r=0}^{\infty}
 \left(1-\frac{z^2}{(r+1)^2}\right)^{1+\vtwo(r+1)}.       \label{eq:fourier-canonical-product}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The complete complex zero-set equivalence is
\lean{rvachevFourier_eq_zero_iff} in \path{FourierProduct.lean}.
The positive-integer limit is
\lean{tendsto_norm_rvachevFourier_div_pow_zero_order} in
\path{RvachevFourierDecay.lean}; its limiting constant is exhibited there as
an exponential, and is therefore strictly positive.  The canonical product is
\lean{rvachevFourier_eq_canonical} in the same facade module, built from
\lean{rvachevFourierProduct_eq_canonical} in \path{SincCanonicalProduct.lean}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Geometric reciprocal-Gamma products}
\label{sec:geometric-reciprocal-gamma}

The Gamma factorization is most naturally formalized from the entire reciprocal
factor
\begin{equation}
 R(z):=\frac1{\Gamma(1+z)}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Geometric reciprocal-Gamma products and the dyadic factor]
For a complex contraction \(q\) satisfying \(\lVert q\rVert<1\), define the
locally uniformly convergent product
\begin{equation}
 G_q(z):=\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}R(q^nz)
 =\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac1{\Gamma(1+q^nz)}.             \label{eq:geometric-reciprocal-gamma}
\end{equation}
The hypothesis includes \(q=0\); no positivity or reality assumption is made.
The reciprocal factor and the geometric product satisfy
\begin{align}
 R(z)R(-z)&=\sinc(\pi z),                                  \label{eq:reciprocal-gamma-reflection}\\
 G_q(0)&=1,\qquad
 G_q(z)=R(z)G_q(qz),                                      \label{eq:geometric-reciprocal-gamma-mahler}\\
 G_q(z)=0&\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists\,n,m\in\N:\ 1+q^nz=-m.                          \label{eq:geometric-reciprocal-gamma-zeros}
\end{align}
In particular there are no hidden zeros in the infinite product.  Define the
Gamma-side total meromorphic representative and the matching sinc product by
\begin{equation}
 \Gamma_{\langle q\rangle}(z):=G_q(z)^{-1},\qquad
 \Phi_q(z):=\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}\sinc(\pi q^nz).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{align}
 \Gamma_{\langle q\rangle}(z)
   &=\Gamma(1+z)\Gamma_{\langle q\rangle}(qz),             \label{eq:geometric-gamma-mahler}\\
 G_q(z)G_q(-z)&=\Phi_q(z).                                \label{eq:geometric-gamma-reflection}
\end{align}

At \(q=1/2\), write \(G_{\rm dy}=G_{1/2}\) and
\(\Gamma_{\rm dy}=G_{\rm dy}^{-1}\).  The geometric sinc product is exactly the
standalone Rvachev product, so for every \(z\in\C\)
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(z)=G_{\rm dy}(z)G_{\rm dy}(-z)
 =\frac1{\Gamma_{\rm dy}(z)\Gamma_{\rm dy}(-z)}.          \label{eq:dyadic-reciprocal-gamma-bridge}
\end{equation}
Moreover \(G_{\rm dy}\) vanishes precisely at the negative integers.  For every
\(n\ge1\),
\begin{align}
 \operatorname{ord}_{z=-n}G_{\rm dy}(z)&=1+\vtwo(n),       \label{eq:dyadic-reciprocal-gamma-order}\\
 \operatorname{mord}_{z=-n}\Gamma_{\rm dy}(z)&=-(1+\vtwo(n)).
                                                                  \label{eq:dyadic-gamma-pole-order}
\end{align}
Thus the positive pole order of \(\Gamma_{\rm dy}\) is \(1+\vtwo(n)\);
Mathlib records it as the displayed negative meromorphic order.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof and exact API]
At formal source checkpoint
\path{71ab6f6728fceb753c88d8b0573077a59acf2682}, the reusable convergence
engine is \path{ScaledInfiniteProducts.lean}.  Its ten public declarations are
\lean{summable_norm_scaled_sub_one},
\lean{hasProdUniformlyOn_scaled},
\lean{multipliableUniformlyOn_scaled},
\lean{hasProdLocallyUniformly_scaled},
\lean{multipliableLocallyUniformly_scaled},
\lean{continuous_tprod_scaled},
\lean{differentiable_tprod_scaled},
\lean{differentiable_tprod_scaled_of_eq_one},
\lean{tprod_scaled_ne_zero}, and
\lean{tprod_scaled_eq_zero_iff}.  The pointwise summability theorem assumes a
normed scalar field, a normed space, a normed-ring target, summability of
\(n\mapsto\lVert a_n\rVert\), and \(f(z)-1=O(z)\) at zero.  The uniform
statements take values in a complete
commutative normed ring with a normed unit and assume continuity of \(f\); the
locally uniform statements additionally assume a locally compact domain.  The
holomorphic statements take values in a complete commutative normed ring with a
normed complex-algebra structure and assume complex differentiability, with
\lean{differentiable_tprod_scaled_of_eq_one} replacing the explicit big-\(O\)
hypothesis by \(f(0)=1\).  The no-hidden-zero statements use a complete
commutative normed ring with a normed unit and a multiplicative norm, but need
neither continuity of \(f\) nor local compactness of the domain.

The specialization is \path{GeometricReciprocalGamma.lean}.  Its twenty-five
public declarations are
\lean{shiftedReciprocalGamma},
\lean{shiftedReciprocalGamma_zero},
\lean{shiftedReciprocalGamma_differentiable},
\lean{shiftedReciprocalGamma_sub_one_isBigO},
\lean{shiftedReciprocalGamma_eq_zero_iff},
\lean{shiftedReciprocalGamma_mul_neg},
\lean{summable_norm_qpow},
\lean{geometricReciprocalGamma},
\lean{geometricReciprocalGammaFactors_multipliable},
\lean{geometricReciprocalGamma_differentiable},
\lean{geometricReciprocalGamma_zero},
\lean{geometricReciprocalGamma_mahler},
\lean{geometricReciprocalGamma_eq_zero_iff},
\lean{geometricGamma},
\lean{geometricGamma_meromorphic},
\lean{geometricGamma_mahler},
\lean{geometricSincProduct},
\lean{geometricReciprocalGamma_mul_neg},
\lean{dyadicReciprocalGamma},
\lean{dyadicGamma},
\lean{dyadicReciprocalGamma_differentiable},
\lean{dyadicReciprocalGamma_zero},
\lean{geometricSincProduct_inv_two},
\lean{dyadicReciprocalGamma_mul_neg}, and
\lean{rvachevFourierProduct_eq_one_div_dyadicGamma_mul}.
Here \(q\in\C\) and \(\lVert q\rVert<1\) are the exact hypotheses on every
generic convergence, holomorphy, Mahler, zero-orbit, meromorphy, and reflection
statement; normalization at zero itself needs no contraction hypothesis.

At every reciprocal integer scale, \path{ReciprocalIntegerGammaZeros.lean}
exposes four public theorems:
\lean{norm_inv_natCast_lt_one},
\lean{geometricReciprocalGamma_inv_natCast_eq_zero_iff},
\lean{geometricReciprocalGamma_inv_natCast_ne_zero_of_nonneg}, and
\lean{geometricGamma_inv_natCast_meromorphic}.  For \(b\ge2\), the entire product
\(G_{1/b}\) vanishes exactly at the embedded strictly negative natural numbers,
independently of \(b\), is nonzero at every embedded nonnegative natural number,
and its pointwise inverse is meromorphic.  This closes the integer-base zero set,
but not the multiplicity of those zeros.

Finally, \path{DyadicGammaOrder.lean} exposes eight public declarations:
\lean{dyadicReciprocalGamma_eq_zero_iff},
\lean{dyadicReciprocalGamma_int_ne_zero_of_nonneg},
\lean{dyadicReciprocalGamma_nat_ne_zero},
\lean{dyadicGamma_meromorphic},
\lean{analyticOrderAt_dyadicReciprocalGamma_int_of_neg},
\lean{analyticOrderAt_dyadicReciprocalGamma_neg_nat},
\lean{meromorphicOrderAt_dyadicGamma_int_of_neg}, and
\lean{meromorphicOrderAt_dyadicGamma_neg_nat}.  The integer forms assume a
strictly negative integer center; the natural-number wrappers assume \(n\ne0\).

There is an important representation boundary.  Mathlib assigns
\lean{Complex.Gamma} the value zero at a classical Gamma pole, and its inverse
also has the totalized value zero there.  Accordingly
\lean{geometricGamma} and \lean{dyadicGamma} are defined as pointwise inverses
of the entire reciprocal products, and a pole is carried by negative
\lean{meromorphicOrderAt}, not by an infinite point value.  At that checkpoint,
the declarations above do not identify these functions with the raw products
\(\prod_n\Gamma(1+q^nz)\) away from their poles, and they assert no arithmetic
Weierstrass regrouping, logarithmic or digamma series, Malmsten or heat-trace
integral, D-finiteness result, reciprocal-Gamma derivative coefficient for the
Thue--Morse tower, or general integer-base collision order.

The post-checkpoint source commit \path{0ba35abd4} closes the reciprocal-Gamma
first-jet and Thue--Morse Gamma-tower tranche in two further modules.
\path{ReciprocalGammaJets.lean} has exactly five public declarations:
\lean{deriv_Gamma_inv_neg_nat},
\lean{hasDerivAt_Gamma_inv_neg_nat},
\lean{hasDerivAt_Gamma_inv_zero},
\lean{analyticOrderAt_Gamma_inv_neg_nat}, and
\lean{tendsto_Gamma_inv_div_add_nat}.  For every \(r\in\N\), they identify
\[
 \left(\Gamma^{-1}\right)'(-r)=(-1)^r r!,\qquad
 \operatorname{ord}_{-r}\Gamma^{-1}=1,
 \qquad
 \frac{\Gamma(s)^{-1}}{s+r}\longrightarrow(-1)^r r!
 \quad(s\to-r,\ s\ne-r),
\]
with \lean{hasDerivAt_Gamma_inv_zero} retaining the origin compatibility name.
These are statements about the entire reciprocal function
\(s\mapsto\Gamma(s)^{-1}\): they do not assign a value or derivative to the raw
Gamma function at one of its poles.

At the same commit, \path{ThueMorseGammaTower.lean} has exactly eight public
declarations:
\lean{hasDerivAt_dirichletMellinContinuation_neg_nat},
\lean{deriv_dirichletMellinContinuation_neg_nat},
\lean{thueMorseGammaLog},
\lean{thueMorseGammaTower},
\lean{thueMorseGammaLog_eq_mellin},
\lean{thueMorseGammaLog_eq_integral},
\lean{thueMorseGammaLog_dyadic}, and
\lean{thueMorseGammaTower_dyadic}.  The first two give, for \(a>0\) and
\(r\in\N\),
\[
 \widetilde D_a'(-r)=(-1)^r r!\,
   \operatorname{Mellin}(t\mapsto e^{-at}\mathcal E(t))(-r).
\]
The two definitions are total for every real \(a\); their Mellin, integral, and
dyadic identification theorems assume exactly \(a>0\).  In particular
\lean{thueMorseGammaLog} is the chosen coordinate
\(L_r(a):=\widetilde D_a'(-r)\), not a proved identity with
\lean{Complex.log} of \lean{thueMorseGammaTower}; the latter is defined as
\(T_r(a):=\exp L_r(a)\).  The formal laws are
\[
 L_r(a)=(-1)^r r!\int_0^\infty t^{-r-1}e^{-at}\mathcal E(t)\,dt,
 \quad
 L_r(a)=2^r\bigl(L_r(a/2)-L_r((a+1)/2)\bigr),
\]
and
\[
 T_r(a)=\left(\frac{T_r(a/2)}{T_r((a+1)/2)}\right)^{2^r}.
\]
The current integrated tree adds the ninth public tower declaration
\lean{ofReal_exp_mpLimit_eq_gammaTower_div}: for \(a,b>0\), the complex
embedding of \lean{Real.exp} of \lean{mpLimit} at \((a,b)\) is
\(T_0(b)/T_0(a)\).  Within this tower tranche, only the differential and
iterated differential ladder in the real parameter \(a\) remains frontier; it
requires a parameter-under-the-Mellin-integral theorem.  The raw Gamma product,
canonical regrouping, logarithmic/digamma expansions, Gamma-product Malmsten
and heat-trace representations, D-finiteness, and general-base collision order listed above
remain separate frontiers.
\end{proof}

The exponent in the canonical product has a finite, base-generic double-counting
origin that is useful independently of sinc products.  For \(b,N,J\in\N\), put
\[
 c_J(b,m):=\#\{j\in\{0,\ldots,J\}:b^j\mid m\}.
\]
If \(q>0\), multiplication by \(q\) identifies the positive multiples of \(q\)
not exceeding \(N\) with \(1,\ldots,\lfloor N/q\rfloor\).  Consequently, for
every function \(f\) into a commutative monoid,
\begin{align}
 \prod_{1\le n\le\lfloor N/q\rfloor}f(qn)
   &=\prod_{\substack{1\le m\le N\\q\mid m}}f(m),
                                                               \label{eq:base-layer-reindexing}\\
 \prod_{j=0}^{J}\prod_{1\le n\le\lfloor N/b^j\rfloor}f(b^jn)
   &=\prod_{m=1}^{N}f(m)^{c_J(b,m)}\qquad(b>0).             \label{eq:base-layer-regrouping}
\end{align}
For \(b>1\) and \(m\ne0\), if the displayed layer range reaches the
multiplicity, that is, if
\(\operatorname{multiplicity}(b,m)\le J\), then
\begin{equation}
 c_J(b,m)=\operatorname{multiplicity}(b,m)+1.               \label{eq:base-layer-count}
\end{equation}
Thus, under the explicit hypothesis
\(\operatorname{multiplicity}(b,m)\le J\) for every \(1\le m\le N\),
\begin{equation}
 \prod_{j=0}^{J}\prod_{1\le n\le\lfloor N/b^j\rfloor}f(b^jn)
 =\prod_{m=1}^{N}f(m)^{\operatorname{multiplicity}(b,m)+1}. \label{eq:base-layer-multiplicity}
\end{equation}
The same three identities hold in every additive commutative monoid, with
products and powers replaced by sums and natural scalar multiples.  In Lean,
the underlying finite-set equality is \lean{filter_dvd_eq_image};
\eqref{eq:base-layer-reindexing} and its additive companion are
\lean{prod_multiples_eq_prod_filter} and
\lean{sum_multiples_eq_sum_filter};
\eqref{eq:base-layer-regrouping} and its companion are
\lean{prod_layers_eq_prod_pow_card} and
\lean{sum_layers_eq_sum_nsmul_card}; and
\eqref{eq:base-layer-count}--\eqref{eq:base-layer-multiplicity} and the final
additive companion are \lean{card_filter_pow_dvd},
\lean{prod_layers_eq_prod_pow_multiplicity}, and
\lean{sum_layers_eq_sum_nsmul_multiplicity}, all in
\path{BaseLayerRegrouping.lean}.  These are finite algebraic identities.  In
particular they assert no infinite-product convergence, and the sufficient
layer-coverage condition must be supplied explicitly rather than inferred by
the API from a bound such as \(N<b^{J+1}\).

\subsection{Total local factorization and exact complex zero jets}
\label{sec:fourier-integer-zero-jets}

The complex sinc identities needed at an integer center are most useful after
their removable denominators have been cleared.  With the convention
\(\sinc z=\sin z/z\), continuously extended at zero, one has for every
\(z\in\C\), every \(n\in\N\), and every \(a\in\C\setminus\{0\}\),
\begin{align}
 z\sinc(\pi z)
   &=\frac{\sin(\pi z)}{\pi},                                      \label{eq:sinc-total-clear}\\
 (n+z)\sinc\!\bigl(\pi(n+z)\bigr)
   &=(-1)^n z\sinc(\pi z),                                        \label{eq:sinc-total-shift}\\
 (an+z)\sinc\!\left(\frac{\pi(an+z)}a\right)
   &=(-1)^n z\sinc\!\left(\frac{\pi z}a\right).                 \label{eq:sinc-total-scaled-shift}
\end{align}
Unlike identities obtained by dividing through by \(z\) or \(n+z\), these
equalities retain the points where either factor vanishes.  They are respectively
\lean{mul_complexSinc_pi}, \lean{mul_complexSinc_pi_add_nat}, and
\lean{mul_complexSinc_pi_div_add_nat} in
\path{IntegerZeroLocalFactorization.lean}; the last theorem allows an arbitrary
nonzero \emph{complex} scale, with no order or reality hypothesis.

Here is the reusable all-order differential principle.  Let \(\mathbb K\) be
any nontrivially normed field, let \(E\) be a normed \(\mathbb K\)-space, and
let \(U:\mathbb K\to E\) be \(C^k\) at zero.  For every \(n,k\in\N\),
\begin{equation}
 D^k\!\left(z\mapsto z^n\mathbin{\cdot}U(z)\right)(0)
 =\begin{cases}
   \bigl(\binom{k}{n}:\mathbb K\bigr)(n!:\mathbb K)\mathbin{\cdot}
      D^{k-n}U(0),&n\leq k,\\[1mm]
   0,&k<n,
  \end{cases}                                                     \label{eq:factored-all-index-jet}
\end{equation}
where \(\mathbin{\cdot}\) denotes scalar action on \(E\).  This is the
vector-valued theorem \lean{iteratedDeriv_pow_smul_zero_eq_ite} in
\path{LeadingJet.lean}.  At the index \(k=n+r\), its conditional disappears:
\begin{equation}
 D^{n+r}\!\left(z\mapsto z^n\mathbin{\cdot}U(z)\right)(0)
 =\bigl(\binom{n+r}{n}:\mathbb K\bigr)(n!:\mathbb K)\mathbin{\cdot}
   D^rU(0).                                                       \label{eq:factored-all-order-jet}
\end{equation}
The vector and scalar forms of \eqref{eq:factored-all-order-jet} are
\lean{iteratedDeriv_pow_smul_zero_add} and
\lean{iteratedDeriv_pow_mul_zero_add}.  If a scalar-valued function \(f\)
agrees with \(z^nU(z)\) merely on a neighborhood of zero, the same formula is
\lean{iteratedDeriv_eq_choose_factorial_mul_of_eventuallyEq_pow_mul}; no
separate regularity hypothesis on \(f\) is needed.  At an arbitrary center
\(z_0\), a vector-valued germ
\(f(z)=(z-z_0)^n\mathbin{\cdot}U(z)\) has the all-index form
\lean{iteratedDeriv_eq_ite_of_eventuallyEq_sub_pow_smul} and the \(n+r\) form
\lean{iteratedDeriv_eq_choose_factorial_smul_of_eventuallyEq_sub_pow_smul};
the scalar specialization is
\lean{iteratedDeriv_eq_choose_factorial_mul_of_eventuallyEq_sub_pow_mul}.

The coefficient is deliberately the product
\(\binom{n+r}{n}n!\), with both natural numbers cast into \(\mathbb K\), not
the quotient \((n+r)!/r!\).  Thus none of these theorems requires factorial
division, characteristic zero, completeness, or analyticity.  In positive
characteristic the displayed scalar can vanish, so the generic statement
identifies the first \emph{potentially} nonzero jet rather than asserting
nonvanishing.  Setting \(r=0\) recovers the compatibility formula
\begin{equation}
 \left.\frac{\dd^n}{\dd z^n}\bigl(z^nU(z)\bigr)\right|_{z=0}
   =n!\,U(0),                                                       \label{eq:factored-leading-jet}
\end{equation}
exported as \lean{iteratedDeriv_pow_mul_zero}; its zero-germ companion is
\lean{iteratedDeriv_eq_factorial_mul_of_eventuallyEq_pow_mul}.

We now apply the total sinc identities to the standalone product
\(\Phi=\lean{rvachevFourierProduct}\).  Put
\begin{equation}
 P_d(w):=\prod_{h=0}^{d-1}
   \sinc\!\left(\frac{\pi w}{2^h}\right),
 \qquad P_0(w)=1.                                                  \label{eq:integer-zero-sinc-prefix}
\end{equation}
For arbitrary \(k,q\in\N\) and \(w\in\C\), the global, denominator-cleared
factorization at the center \(2^kq\) is
\begin{equation}
 (2^kq+w)^{k+1}\Phi(2^kq+w)
 =(-1)^q w^{k+1}P_{k+1}(w)
   \Phi\!\left(\frac q2+\frac{w}{2^{k+1}}\right).                  \label{eq:dyadic-center-total-factorization}
\end{equation}
This is
\lean{rvachevFourierProduct_two_pow_mul_add_factorization} in
\path{IntegerZeroLocalFactorization.lean}.  It includes even \(q\), when the
tail can retain further zero multiplicity, and also the degenerate center
\(q=0\).  If \(q\) is odd, its sign is exactly \(-1\); the corresponding
specialization is
\lean{rvachevFourierProduct_two_pow_mul_odd_add_factorization}.

For a positive integer \(m\), let
\(d=\vtwo(m)+1\) and let \(q=m/2^{\vtwo(m)}\) be its canonical odd part.  Then
\begin{equation}
 (m+w)^d\Phi(m+w)
 =-w^dP_d(w)\Phi\!\left(\frac q2+\frac{w}{2^d}\right),             \label{eq:natural-center-total-factorization}
\end{equation}
which is \lean{rvachevFourierProduct_nat_add_factorization}.  The fully signed
form is just as clean.  For \(m\in\Z\setminus\{0\}\), set
\begin{equation}
 d_m:=\vtwo(|m|)+1,\qquad
 q_m:=\frac{|m|}{2^{\vtwo(|m|)}},\qquad
 \sigma_m:=\sgn(m).
\end{equation}
Then \(q_m\) is odd and, for every complex \(w\),
\begin{equation}
 (m+w)^{d_m}\Phi(m+w)
 =-w^{d_m}P_{d_m}(w)
   \Phi\!\left(\frac{q_m}{2}+\sigma_m\frac{w}{2^{d_m}}\right).     \label{eq:integer-center-total-factorization}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{rvachevFourierProduct_int_add_factorization}; the sign of the
integer center occurs only in the tail coordinate.  Equations
\eqref{eq:dyadic-center-total-factorization}--\eqref{eq:integer-center-total-factorization}
are polynomially cleared identities on all of \(\C\), not punctured-neighborhood
statements.

The remaining analytic argument can therefore be packaged into a single local
unit.  The product \(\Phi\) is entire by
\lean{rvachevFourierProduct_differentiable}, and, for every odd natural \(q\),
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(q/2)\ne0,                                                    \label{eq:odd-half-integer-nonzero}
\end{equation}
by \lean{rvachevFourierProduct_nat_div_two_ne_zero_of_odd}.  Define the total
cofactor
\begin{equation}
 U_m(w):=
 -\frac{P_{d_m}(w)
   \Phi\!\left(q_m/2+\sigma_m w/2^{d_m}\right)}{(m+w)^{d_m}}.      \label{eq:integer-zero-local-cofactor}
\end{equation}
The Lean definition \lean{integerZeroLocalCofactor} is total for every integer
\(m\).  For \(m\ne0\) and \(m+w\ne0\),
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(m+w)=w^{d_m}U_m(w),                                         \label{eq:integer-zero-local-factorization}
\end{equation}
by \lean{rvachevFourierProduct_int_add_eq_pow_mul_cofactor}; the same equality
holds as a germ at \(w=0\) by
\lean{rvachevFourierProduct_int_add_eventuallyEq_pow_mul_cofactor}.  In the
original coordinate this is the centered germ
\[
 \Phi(z)=(z-m)^{d_m}U_m(z-m)
 \quad\hbox{near }z=m,
\]
exported as
\lean{rvachevFourierProduct_int_eventuallyEq_sub_pow_mul_cofactor}.

For every integer parameter \(m\), with no parity or nonzero hypothesis, the
total quotient \(U_m\) is analytic at every point \(w_0\) satisfying
\(m+w_0\ne0\), by
\lean{integerZeroLocalCofactor_analyticAt_of_add_ne}.  For nonzero \(m\), its
local-origin specialization is \lean{integerZeroLocalCofactor_analyticAt}.
Its exact central value is
\begin{equation}
 U_m(0)=-\frac{\Phi(q_m/2)}{m^{d_m}}\ne0.                          \label{eq:integer-zero-unit-value}
\end{equation}
These three facts are \lean{integerZeroLocalCofactor_analyticAt},
\lean{integerZeroLocalCofactor_zero}, and
\lean{integerZeroLocalCofactor_zero_ne}, respectively.  More precisely, the
middle theorem is the all-integer totalized identity
\[
 U_m(0)=
 -\frac{\Phi\!\left(\operatorname{divMaxPow}(|m|,2)/2\right)}
 {m^{\operatorname{padicValNat}(2,|m|)+1}};
\]
for \(m\ne0\) it reduces to \eqref{eq:integer-zero-unit-value}, and the last
theorem makes it an analytic unit.

Combining the germ factorization, the nonzero unit, and
\eqref{eq:factored-leading-jet} gives the exact complex multiplicity and its
entire leading jet:
\begin{align}
 \operatorname{ord}_{z=m}\Phi(z)&=d_m,                             \label{eq:integer-zero-complex-order}\\
 \Phi^{(j)}(m)&=0 &&(0\le j<d_m),                                  \label{eq:integer-zero-lower-jets}\\
 \Phi^{(d_m)}(m)
   &=-\frac{d_m!\,\Phi(q_m/2)}{m^{d_m}}\ne0.                       \label{eq:integer-zero-leading-jet}
\end{align}
In fact the division-free all-order identity is, for every \(r\in\N\),
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \Phi^{(d_m+r)}(m)
 =\binom{d_m+r}{d_m}d_m!\,U_m^{(r)}(0).
 \quad}                                                           \label{eq:integer-zero-all-higher-jets}
\end{equation}
The order statement is
\lean{analyticOrderAt_rvachevFourierProduct_int}; the lower derivatives are
\lean{iteratedDeriv_rvachevFourierProduct_int_eq_zero_of_lt}; the displayed
all-order formula is
\lean{iteratedDeriv_rvachevFourierProduct_int_add_order}; the displayed
leading coefficient combines its \(r=0\) case with
\lean{integerZeroLocalCofactor_zero} and is exported as
\lean{iteratedDeriv_rvachevFourierProduct_int}; and its nonvanishing is also
exported directly as
\lean{iteratedDeriv_rvachevFourierProduct_int_ne_zero}, all in
\path{IntegerZeroAnalyticOrder.lean}.  This strengthens the real
positive-integer limit in \Cref{thm:fourier-zeros}: it is a signed-integer,
complex-local theorem with the first surviving derivative evaluated exactly.

\subsection{Finite weighted scale arithmetic behind the zero orders}

The valuation in \eqref{eq:fourier-zero-order} belongs to a more general
finite additive calculus; neither primality of the scale base nor an analytic
infinite product is needed for this layer.  Let \((A,+,0)\) be an additive
commutative monoid, let \(w:\N\to A\), and define the inclusive weighted
prefix
\begin{equation}
 P_w(n):=\sum_{h=0}^{n}w(h).                              \label{eq:weighted-inclusive-prefix}
\end{equation}
It satisfies
\begin{align}
 P_w(0)&=w(0),\qquad
 P_w(n+1)=P_w(n)+w(n+1),                                 \label{eq:weighted-prefix-boundaries}\\
 P_w(m+n)&=\sum_{h=0}^{m-1}w(h)+P_{w^{\langle m\rangle}}(n),
 \qquad w^{\langle m\rangle}(h):=w(m+h).                \label{eq:weighted-prefix-split}
\end{align}
Empty sums have their usual value.  More generally, if \(S\) is finite,
\(a:S\to\N\), and \(a(x)<H\) for every \(x\in S\), then
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{x\in S}P_w(a(x))
 =\sum_{h=0}^{H-1}\#\{x\in S:h\le a(x)\}\odot w(h),     \label{eq:weighted-layer-cake}
\end{equation}
where \(q\odot y\) means the sum of \(q\) copies of \(y\).  This finite
layer-cake identity requires no subtraction, order, or multiplication on
\(A\).

For a natural base \(b\), put
\begin{equation}
 \nu_b(n):=\operatorname{padicValNat}(b,n),\qquad
 W_{b,w}(n):=P_w(\nu_b(n))
            =\sum_{h=0}^{\nu_b(n)}w(h).                  \label{eq:weighted-scale-multiplicity}
\end{equation}
Thus, for \(b>1\), \(n>0\), and \(k\in\N\),
\begin{align}
 W_{b,w}(b^kn)
   &=\sum_{h=0}^{k-1}w(h)+W_{b,w^{\langle k\rangle}}(n), \label{eq:weighted-scale-shift}\\
 W_{b,w}(bn)
   &=w(0)+W_{b,w^{\langle1\rangle}}(n).                 \label{eq:weighted-scale-one-shift}
\end{align}
For the divisibility form, the optimal algebraic hypothesis is only
\(b\ne1\).  If \(n>0\) and \(\nu_b(n)<H\), then
\begin{equation}
 W_{b,w}(n)=\sum_{\substack{0\le h<H\\b^h\mid n}}w(h).  \label{eq:weighted-scale-divisors-bounded}
\end{equation}
Since \(\nu_b(n)<n\), the canonical choice \(H=n\) gives
\begin{equation}
 W_{b,w}(n)=\sum_{\substack{0\le h<n\\b^h\mid n}}w(h).  \label{eq:weighted-scale-divisors}
\end{equation}
Summing over the positive integers through \(N\) and exchanging the two
finite sums yields
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{m=0}^{N-1}W_{b,w}(m+1)
 =\sum_{h=0}^{N-1}\bigl(N\mathbin{\mathrm{div}}b^h\bigr)\odot w(h)
 \qquad(b\ne1),                                         \label{eq:weighted-scale-cumulative}
\end{equation}
where \(\mathrm{div}\) denotes natural-number quotient.  For \(b>1\) this
is the familiar floor \(\lfloor N/b^h\rfloor\).

Pascal weights now give the hockey-stick identities
\begin{align}
 P_{h\mapsto\binom{h+r}{r}}(n)&=\binom{n+r+1}{r+1},      \label{eq:weighted-prefix-shifted-pascal}\\
 P_{h\mapsto\binom hr}(n)&=\binom{n+1}{r+1}.             \label{eq:weighted-prefix-pascal}
\end{align}
Consequently, with no restriction on \(b,n,r\),
\begin{equation}
 W_{b,h\mapsto\binom hr}(n)
 =\binom{\nu_b(n)+1}{r+1},                              \label{eq:weighted-scale-pascal}
\end{equation}
and, for \(b\ne1\),
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{m=0}^{N-1}\binom{\nu_b(m+1)+1}{r+1}
 =\sum_{h=0}^{N-1}\bigl(N\mathbin{\mathrm{div}}b^h\bigr)\binom hr.
                                                               \label{eq:weighted-scale-pascal-cumulative}
\end{equation}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The definition \eqref{eq:weighted-inclusive-prefix}, its zero and successor
laws, and its shifted splitting law are
\lean{inclusivePrefixSum}, \lean{inclusivePrefixSum_zero},
\lean{inclusivePrefixSum_succ}, and \lean{inclusivePrefixSum_add}.
The monoid-valued Fubini identity \eqref{eq:weighted-layer-cake} is
\lean{sum_inclusivePrefixSum_eq_sum_filter_card_nsmul}.

The scale definition \eqref{eq:weighted-scale-multiplicity} is
\lean{weightedScaleMultiplicity}.  Equations
\eqref{eq:weighted-scale-shift} and \eqref{eq:weighted-scale-one-shift} are
\lean{weightedScaleMultiplicity_base_pow_mul} and
\lean{weightedScaleMultiplicity_base_mul}.  The bounded and canonical
divisibility sums \eqref{eq:weighted-scale-divisors-bounded} and
\eqref{eq:weighted-scale-divisors} are
\lean{weightedScaleMultiplicity_eq_sum_filter_pow_dvd_of_lt} and
\lean{weightedScaleMultiplicity_eq_sum_filter_pow_dvd}; the cumulative
identity \eqref{eq:weighted-scale-cumulative} is
\lean{sum_range_weightedScaleMultiplicity}.

Finally, \eqref{eq:weighted-prefix-shifted-pascal} and
\eqref{eq:weighted-prefix-pascal} are
\lean{inclusivePrefixSum_add_choose} and \lean{inclusivePrefixSum_choose}.
Their valuation specialization and cumulative floor sum are
\lean{weightedScaleMultiplicity_choose} and
\lean{sum_range_padicValNat_choose}.  All declarations are in
\path{WeightedScaleMultiplicity.lean}.
\end{proof}

Lean totalizes the boundary cases:
\(\nu_b(0)=\nu_0(n)=\nu_1(n)=0\), and natural division by zero is zero.
Accordingly, the resulting \(W\)-values at those degenerate inputs are
algebraic conventions.  More importantly, this subsection proves finite
arithmetic identities only.  Formula \eqref{eq:weighted-scale-pascal}
supplies the binomial expression proposed for higher-rank Rvachev products,
but it does not assert convergence of such a product or identify its analytic
zero order.  Those analytic bridges remain separate obligations.

On the real axis the origin is the unique global point of unit modulus:
\begin{equation}
 |\widehat{\Up}(x)|=1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad x=0,
 \qquad x\ne0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad |\widehat{\Up}(x)|<1.
                                                               \label{eq:fourier-global-peak}
\end{equation}
These are \lean{norm_rvachevFourier_eq_one_iff} and
\lean{norm_rvachevFourier_lt_one_of_ne_zero}.  On each positive real lobe
the sign and peak are also exact.  If \(m\in\N\) and \(m<x<m+1\), then
\begin{equation}
 \widehat{\Up}(x)=\varepsilon_m|\widehat{\Up}(x)|,        \label{eq:fourier-lobe-sign}
\end{equation}
by \lean{rvachevFourier_eq_thueMorse_sign_mul_norm}.  If moreover \(m\ge1\),
there is exactly one point of \((m,m+1)\) maximizing the modulus over that
open lobe and exactly one point of the reflected lobe \((-(m+1),-m)\)
maximizing it there.  These are
\lean{existsUnique_isMaxOn_rvachevFourier_lobe} and
\lean{existsUnique_isMaxOn_rvachevFourier_neg_lobe}.  All five facade
declarations are in \path{RvachevFourierDecay.lean}.

Globally, the origin is the unique real frequency of unit modulus:
\begin{equation}
 \left|\widehat{\Up}(x)\right|\le1,
 \qquad
 \left|\widehat{\Up}(x)\right|=1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad x=0.
                                                               \label{eq:fourier-strict-global-peak}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, the first inequality is strict whenever \(x\ne0\).  These statements are
\lean{norm_rvachevFourier_le_one},
\lean{norm_rvachevFourier_lt_one_of_ne_zero}, and
\lean{norm_rvachevFourier_eq_one_iff} in \path{RvachevFourierDecay.lean}.

\subsection{Rapid decay and inversion}

For every \(N\in\N\) there is a constant \(C_N>0\) such that
\begin{equation}
 |\xi|^N|\widehat{\Up}(\xi)|\le C_N\qquad(\xi\in\R),     \label{eq:schwartz-decay}
\end{equation}
and the companion formal theorem gives the corresponding polynomially weighted bound for
every iterated real-frequency derivative.  Fourier inversion holds in the exact form
\begin{equation}
 \Up(x)=\int_\R\widehat{\Up}(\xi)\e^{2\pi\ii x\xi}\dd\xi. \label{eq:fourier-inversion}
\end{equation}
These are \lean{rvachevFourier_real_rapidDecay},
\lean{rvachevFourier_real_iteratedDeriv_rapidDecay}, and
\lean{rvachev_fourier_inversion_analytic} in
\path{PoissonSummation.lean} and \path{FourierAnalytic.lean}.

\subsection{Poisson summation and partitions of unity}

For \(u>0\) and \(t\in\R\), the formal Poisson identity is
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k\in\Z}\Up(t+uk)
 =\frac1u\sum_{k\in\Z}\widehat{\Up}(k/u)
   \e^{2\pi\ii kt/u}.                                     \label{eq:poisson-up}
\end{equation}
It is \lean{rvachev_poisson_summation}.  Combining it with
\eqref{eq:fourier-integer-zeros} gives, for every positive integer \(m\),
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k\in\Z}\Up\!\left(t+\frac{k}{m}\right)=m,          \label{eq:partition-unity}
\end{equation}
and in particular
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k\in\Z}\Up(t+k)=1.                                \label{eq:integer-partition}
\end{equation}
These are \lean{rvachev_partition_one_over_nat} and
\lean{rvachev_partition_unity} in \path{PoissonSummation.lean}.

\subsection{Banach-free cyclic aliasing}
\label{sec:summable-cyclic-alias}

\begin{theorem}[Finite-range twists and cyclic aliasing]
\label{thm:summable-cyclic-alias}
The convergence mechanism behind a root-of-unity filter does not require a
norm.  Let \(R\) be any scalar type, let \(B\) be any index type, let \(I\)
be finite, and let \(E\) be a complete uniform additive commutative group
with a distributive \(R\)-action such that multiplication by each fixed
scalar is continuous.  If \(a:B\to E\) is summable, \(\ell:B\to I\), and
\(c:I\to R\), then
\begin{equation}
 b\longmapsto c(\ell(b))\,a(b)
 \quad\text{is summable}.                                \label{eq:finite-range-scalar-twist}
\end{equation}
Thus a scalar multiplier taking only finitely many values preserves
unconditional summability.

Separately, let \(E\) be a complete Hausdorff uniform additive commutative
group, write \(\bar n\) for the class of \(n\in\Z\) in
\(\operatorname{ZMod}(q)\), and define
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal R_r(a):=
 \sum_{\substack{n\in\Z\\\bar n=r}}a(n).                \label{eq:int-residue-tsum}
\end{equation}
For every \(q\in\N\), including Lean's convention
\(\operatorname{ZMod}(0)=\Z\), the family
\(r\mapsto\mathcal R_r(a)\) has sum \(\sum_{n\in\Z}a(n)\).
In particular, for \(q>0\),
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{r\in\Z/q\Z}\mathcal R_r(a)=\sum_{n\in\Z}a(n).
                                                               \label{eq:sum-int-residue-tsum}
\end{equation}

Suppose in addition that \(E\) is a complex module and multiplication by each
fixed complex scalar is continuous.  Let \(\chi_q\) be the standard additive
character of \(\Z/q\Z\).  For \(q>0\), put
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal A_k(a):=
 \sum_{n\in\Z}\chi_q(-\bar n k)\,a(n).                  \label{eq:int-fourier-tsum}
\end{equation}
Every twisted series in \eqref{eq:int-fourier-tsum} is summable.  With the
unnormalized discrete Fourier transform
\[
 (\mathcal D_qf)(k):=
 \sum_{r\in\Z/q\Z}\chi_q(-rk)f(r),
\]
one has the exact forward and inverse identities
\begin{align}
 \mathcal D_q\bigl(\mathcal R_\bullet(a)\bigr)(k)
   &=\mathcal A_k(a),                                    \label{eq:dft-int-residue-tsum}\\
 \mathcal R_r(a)
   &=\frac1q\sum_{k\in\Z/q\Z}
       \chi_q(kr)\,\mathcal A_k(a).                      \label{eq:int-residue-dft-average}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The finite-range convergence principle \eqref{eq:finite-range-scalar-twist}
is \lean{summable_finite_range_smul}.  The two sums are
\lean{intResidueTsum} and \lean{intFourierTsum}; character twisting is
\lean{intFourierSummand_summable}.  The fiberwise summation statement and
its finite-modulus consequence are \lean{hasSum_intResidueTsum} and
\lean{sum_intResidueTsum}.  The forward transform and normalized inverse
are \lean{dft_intResidueTsum} and
\lean{intResidueTsum_eq_dft_average}.  All eight declarations are in
\path{SummableCyclicAlias.lean}.

The proof partitions the bilateral family into its residue fibers, observes
that each cyclic character is constant on a fiber, and applies the finite
DFT and its inverse.  Summability of \(a\) is the only convergence
hypothesis; norm-summability is not assumed.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Moment expansion of the Fourier transform}

With
\begin{equation}
 c_n=\int_{-1}^{1}x^{2n}\Up(x)\dd x.                    \label{eq:c-def}
\end{equation}
the exact entire series is
\begin{equation}
 \widehat{\Up}(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}
   \frac{(-1)^n c_n}{(2n)!}(2\pi z)^{2n}.                  \label{eq:fourier-moment-series}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{rvachevFourier_eq_momentSeries} in
\path{AnalyticMoments.lean}.

\section{The global extension, derivatives, flatness, and inverse}
\label{sec:global}

\subsection{The signed extension and all iterated derivatives}

The translate series \eqref{eq:global-extension} is locally finite.  It agrees with the
bounded \(F\) on \([0,1]\), vanishes on \((-\infty,0]\), and satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal F'(x)=2\mathcal F(2x)\qquad(x\in\R).             \label{eq:global-diffeq-again}
\end{equation}
These facts are \lean{extendedFabius_eq_fabiusReal},
\lean{extendedFabius_eq_zero_of_nonpos}, and
\lean{extendedFabius_hasDerivAt} in \path{GlobalExtension.lean}.

Iterating the dilation law gives the exact identities
\begin{align}
 \mathcal F^{(m)}(x)
 &=2^{\binom{m+1}{2}}\mathcal F(2^m x),                    \label{eq:global-derivatives}\\
 \Up^{(m)}(x)
 &=2^{\binom{m+1}{2}}\mathcal F\!\left(2^m(x+1)\right)
 \qquad(-1\le x\le1).                                      \label{eq:up-global-derivatives}
\end{align}
They are \lean{iteratedDeriv_extendedFabius} and
\lean{iteratedDeriv_rvachev}.  The global extension obeys
\begin{equation}
 |\mathcal F(x)|\le1,                                      \label{eq:global-bound}
\end{equation}
by \lean{abs_extendedFabius_le_one}.  Hence, for all \(m\in\N\) and \(x\in\R\),
\begin{equation}
 |F^{(m)}(x)|\le2^{\binom{m+1}{2}},                        \label{eq:sharp-derivative-bound}
\end{equation}
and this bound is attained at \(x=2^{-m}\):
\begin{equation}
 F^{(m)}(2^{-m})=2^{\binom{m+1}{2}}.                       \label{eq:sharp-derivative-attained}
\end{equation}
The exact bound, attainment, and supremum statements are
\lean{abs_iteratedDeriv_fabiusReal_le},
\lean{iteratedDeriv_fabiusReal_inv_two_pow_all}, and
\lean{isGreatest_abs_iteratedDeriv_fabiusReal_all} in
\path{BoundedDerivatives.lean}.

\subsection{Normalized Volterra calculus and exact Fabius primitives}
\label{sec:normalized-volterra}

Repeated integration is most useful here in a form that remembers the base point, works for
either orientation of the endpoints, and does not introduce a new normalization at order
zero.  Let \(E\) be a real normed vector space, \(f:\R\to E\), and \(a\in\R\).  Write
\begin{align}
 (V_af)(x)&:=\int_a^x f(t)\dd t,                                      \label{eq:volterra-first}\\
 L_a^0f&:=f, & L_a^{n+1}f&:=V_a(L_a^nf),                              \label{eq:volterra-literal}\\
 J_a^0f(x)&:=f(x), &
 J_a^{n+1}f(x)&:=\int_a^x\frac{(x-t)^n}{n!}\,f(t)\dd t.               \label{eq:volterra-normalized}
\end{align}
Scalar multiplication in \(E\) is understood in the last display.  These are oriented
interval integrals: no hypothesis such as \(a\le x\) occurs.  In Lean they are the three
public definitions \lean{volterraPrimitive}, \lean{iteratedPrimitive}, and
\lean{normalizedVolterra} in \path{NormalizedVolterra.lean}.

\begin{theorem}[Literal primitive ladder]
\label{thm:literal-volterra-ladder}
For every \(m,n\in\N\), literal iteration satisfies
\begin{align}
 L_a^0f&=f, &
 L_a^{n+1}f&=V_a(L_a^nf),                                      \label{eq:literal-volterra-rec}\\
 L_a^{m+n}f&=L_a^m(L_a^nf), &
 L_a^{n+1}f(a)&=0.                                             \label{eq:literal-volterra-semigroup}
\end{align}
If \(E\) is complete and \(f\) is continuous, then every \(L_a^nf\) is continuous and
\begin{align}
 D(L_a^{n+1}f)(x)&=L_a^nf(x),                                  \label{eq:literal-volterra-ftc}\\
 D^k(L_a^{k+m}f)&=L_a^mf,                                      \label{eq:literal-volterra-cancel}\\
 D^k(L_a^nf)&=L_a^{n-k}f\qquad(k\le n).                        \label{eq:literal-volterra-bounded-cancel}
\end{align}
The derivative in \eqref{eq:literal-volterra-ftc} exists in the strict sense, and
\(L_a^nf\) is \(C^n\).
\end{theorem}

The four algebraic identities are respectively
\lean{iteratedPrimitive_zero}, \lean{iteratedPrimitive_succ},
\lean{iteratedPrimitive_add}, and \lean{iteratedPrimitive_succ_self}.  Continuity is
\lean{continuous_volterraPrimitive} and \lean{continuous_iteratedPrimitive}.  The strict
and ordinary pointwise FTC statements, and its equality-of-functions form, are
\lean{iteratedPrimitive_succ_hasStrictDerivAt},
\lean{iteratedPrimitive_succ_hasDerivAt}, and
\lean{deriv_iteratedPrimitive_succ}.  The two cancellation formulas and the smoothness
statement are \lean{iteratedDeriv_iteratedPrimitive_add},
\lean{iteratedDeriv_iteratedPrimitive}, and \lean{contDiff_iteratedPrimitive}.

\begin{theorem}[Canonical Cauchy kernel]
\label{thm:normalized-volterra-calculus}
The normalized kernel has the total order-zero and positive-order rules
\begin{align}
 J_a^0f(x)&=f(x), &
 J_a^{n+1}f(x)&=\int_a^x\frac{(x-t)^n}{n!}f(t)\dd t,             \label{eq:J-unfold}\\
 J_a^1f(x)&=\int_a^x f(t)\dd t, &
 J_a^{n+1}f(a)&=0.                                             \label{eq:J-one-base}
\end{align}
If \(f=g\) on the closed unoriented interval between \(a\) and \(x\), then
\(J_a^nf(x)=J_a^ng(x)\).  Real scalar multiplication commutes with \(J_a^n\), and any
finite sum of interval-integrable inputs may be passed through it.

If \(E\) is complete and \(f\) is continuous, then literal iteration and the kernel agree,
\begin{equation}
 L_a^nf=J_a^nf,                                               \label{eq:literal-equals-J}
\end{equation}
and hence
\begin{align}
 D(J_a^{n+1}f)(x)&=J_a^nf(x) &&\text{strictly},               \label{eq:J-strict-ftc}\\
 D^k(J_a^{k+m}f)&=J_a^mf,                                    \label{eq:J-cancel}\\
 J_a^{m+n}f&=J_a^m(J_a^nf).                                  \label{eq:J-semigroup}
\end{align}
Moreover \(J_a^nf\) is \(C^n\).
\end{theorem}

The four unfolding and base-point declarations are
\lean{normalizedVolterra_zero}, \lean{normalizedVolterra_succ},
\lean{normalizedVolterra_one}, and \lean{normalizedVolterra_succ_self}; locality,
scalar compatibility, and finite additivity are \lean{normalizedVolterra_congr},
\lean{normalizedVolterra_smul}, and \lean{normalizedVolterra_finsetSum}.
Equation \eqref{eq:literal-equals-J} is
\lean{iteratedPrimitive_eq_normalizedVolterra}.  The remaining conclusions are, in the
order displayed, \lean{normalizedVolterra_succ_hasStrictDerivAt},
\lean{deriv_normalizedVolterra_succ},
\lean{iteratedDeriv_normalizedVolterra_add},
\lean{normalizedVolterra_add}, and \lean{contDiff_normalizedVolterra}.

The normalization is exactly covariant under every real affine map
\(A(t)=ct+d\):
\begin{align}
 J_{ca+d}^n f(cx+d)
 &=c^n J_a^n(f\circ A)(x),                                  \label{eq:volterra-affine}\\
 J_a^n(f\circ A)(x)
 &=(c^n)^{-1}J_{ca+d}^n f(cx+d)
 \qquad(c\ne0).                                             \label{eq:volterra-comp-affine}
\end{align}
The first, division-free form includes negative scales, whose sign is handled by the
oriented integral, and even \(c=0\); it also includes order zero.  Neither form assumes
integrability or completeness.  They are \lean{normalizedVolterra_affine} and
\lean{normalizedVolterra_comp_affine}.

Changing the base point also has an exact finite form.  If \(f\) is interval integrable
on the two oriented pieces from \(a\) to \(b\) and from \(b\) to \(x\), then
\begin{equation}
 J_a^n f(x)
 =J_b^n f(x)
  +\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{(x-b)^k}{k!}\,J_a^{n-k}f(b).
 \label{eq:volterra-basepoint-shift}
\end{equation}
No ordering of \(a,b,x\) is required.  At \(n=0\) the sum is empty and both Volterra
terms reduce to \(f(x)\).  This is \lean{normalizedVolterra_basepoint_shift}.  If, in
addition, \(f\) vanishes on the open unoriented interval between \(b\) and \(x\), then
the local positive-order tail is zero and
\begin{equation}
 J_a^{n+1}f(x)
 =\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(x-b)^k}{k!}\,J_a^{n+1-k}f(b).
 \label{eq:volterra-zero-tail-taylor}
\end{equation}
Only interval integrability from \(a\) to \(b\) is assumed in this corollary; no value
condition is imposed at either endpoint, and the vanishing hypothesis is vacuous when
\(b=x\).  The declaration is
\lean{normalizedVolterra_succ_eq_taylor_of_eq_zero}.

The hypothesis boundary is therefore sharp: affine covariance, the base-point identities,
and the direct kernel algebra take values in an arbitrary real normed vector space.
Completeness is used only for the strict-FTC and smooth literal-iteration layer, its
identification with the direct kernel, and the Taylor reconstruction below.  The four
affine and base-point declarations just named are post-mainline additions checked at
code checkpoint \texttt{e109088ed}.

The finite algebra of polynomial weights is especially clean when written with rising
factorials.  Put
\[
 n^{\overline r}:=n(n+1)\cdots(n+r-1),\qquad n^{\overline0}:=1,
\]
and write \(P^{[r]}\) for the \(r\)-th Hasse derivative of a real polynomial \(P\).

\begin{theorem}[Kernel raising, polynomial commutator, and Taylor reconstruction]
\label{thm:volterra-polynomial-taylor}
For every \(n,r\in\N\) and fixed endpoint \(x\),
\begin{equation}
 J_a^n\bigl((x-\mathord\cdot)^r f\bigr)(x)
 =n^{\overline r}J_a^{n+r}f(x).                              \label{eq:J-kernel-raising}
\end{equation}
This includes \(n=0\): the rising factorial retains the identity when \(r=0\) and
annihilates the positive shifts when \(r>0\).  If \(f\) is interval integrable between
\(a\) and \(x\), then
\begin{align}
 J_a^n(Pf)(x)
 &=\sum_{r=0}^{\operatorname{natdeg}P}
   (-1)^r n^{\overline r}P^{[r]}(x)J_a^{n+r}f(x),            \label{eq:J-polynomial}\\
 J_a^n(t^pf(t))(x)
 &=\sum_{r=0}^{p}(-1)^r\binom pr n^{\overline r}x^{p-r}
   J_a^{n+r}f(x).                                           \label{eq:J-monomial}
\end{align}
Here the second left side means \(J_a^n(t\mapsto t^pf(t))(x)\).  The formulas have
separate positive-order companions in which \(n\) is written \(n+1\); those companions
have the same hypotheses and retain the literal Cauchy-kernel order.

Finally, if \(E\) is complete and \(g:\R\to E\) is \(C^{n+1}\), then
\begin{equation}
 J_a^{n+1}(D^{n+1}g)(x)
 =g(x)-\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(x-a)^k}{k!}D^kg(a).              \label{eq:J-taylor-remainder}
\end{equation}
In particular, if \(D^kg(a)=0\) for \(0\le k\le n\), then the left side is exactly
\(g(x)\).
\end{theorem}

The positive-order and all-order kernel identities are
\lean{normalizedVolterra_succ_kernel_pow} and
\lean{normalizedVolterra_kernel_pow}.  The Hasse-derivative formulas are
\lean{normalizedVolterra_succ_polynomial} and
\lean{normalizedVolterra_polynomial}; their monomial specializations are
\lean{normalizedVolterra_succ_monomial} and
\lean{normalizedVolterra_monomial}.  Taylor's formula and its zero-jet reconstruction
are \lean{normalizedVolterra_succ_iteratedDeriv_eq_sub_taylor} and
\lean{normalizedVolterra_succ_iteratedDeriv_eq_of_zero_jet}.  Thus every declaration in
the generic module has a literal mathematical counterpart above, including the order-zero
and endpoint cases.

For a real order \(\alpha\), define the total oriented operator
\begin{equation}
 (I_{a+}^{\alpha}f)(x)
 :=\int_a^x\frac{(x-t)^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}f(t)\dd t.
 \label{eq:fractional-volterra}
\end{equation}
Here the real power is Lean's total real-power convention.  Its classical
Riemann--Liouville interpretation below is restricted to \(0<\alpha\) and \(a\le x\).

\begin{theorem}[Positive-real fractional Volterra foundation, covariance, and order raising]
\label{thm:fractional-volterra-foundation}
Let \(E\) be a real normed vector space and \(f,g:\R\to E\).  For arbitrary real
\(\alpha,a,x,c,d\), the operator vanishes at its base point, depends only on the input on
the closed unoriented interval between \(a\) and \(x\), and satisfies
\begin{align}
 I_{a+}^{\alpha}(cf)(x)&=cI_{a+}^{\alpha}f(x),
 & I_{a+}^{1}f(x)&=\int_a^x f(t)\dd t.                       \label{eq:fractional-volterra-algebra}
\end{align}
For every \(n\in\N\), without endpoint-order or regularity assumptions,
\begin{equation}
 I_{a+}^{n+1}f(x)=J_a^{n+1}f(x).                             \label{eq:fractional-volterra-natural}
\end{equation}
If \(0<\alpha\), \(a\le x\), and \(f\) and \(g\) are continuous on \([a,x]\), then
the two fractional kernels are interval-integrable and
\begin{equation}
 I_{a+}^{\alpha}(f+g)(x)
 =I_{a+}^{\alpha}f(x)+I_{a+}^{\alpha}g(x).                  \label{eq:fractional-volterra-add-input}
\end{equation}
More generally, let \(a\le b\), \(a\le x\), and let \(h\) be interval-integrable on
\([a,x]\).  If \(h=0\) on the open tail \((b,x)\), then
\begin{equation}
 \int_a^x h(t)\dd t=\int_a^{\min(x,b)}h(t)\dd t.
 \label{eq:interval-integral-support-cutoff}
\end{equation}
If \(0<\alpha\), \(f\) is continuous on \([a,x]\), and \(f=0\) on \((b,x)\), this
specializes to
\begin{equation}
 I_{a+}^{\alpha}f(x)
 =\int_a^{\min(x,b)}\frac{(x-t)^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}f(t)\dd t.
 \label{eq:fractional-volterra-support-cutoff}
\end{equation}
For \(0<\alpha\), \(0<\beta\), and \(s\le x\), the shifted real beta kernel is
interval-integrable.  If \(s<x\), its raw value is
\begin{equation}
 \int_s^x(x-u)^{\alpha-1}(u-s)^{\beta-1}\dd u
 =(x-s)^{\alpha+\beta-1}
   \frac{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(\beta)}{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}.
 \label{eq:fractional-volterra-beta-kernel}
\end{equation}
Its Gamma-normalized form is
\begin{equation}
 \int_s^x
 \frac{(x-u)^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}
 \frac{(u-s)^{\beta-1}}{\Gamma(\beta)}\dd u
 =\frac{(x-s)^{\alpha+\beta-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}.
 \label{eq:fractional-volterra-normalized-beta-kernel}
\end{equation}
If, moreover, \(E\) is complete, \(a\le x\), and \(f\) is continuous on
\([a,x]\), then
\begin{equation}
 I_{a+}^{\alpha+\beta}f(x)
 =I_{a+}^{\alpha}\!\left[u\mapsto I_{a+}^{\beta}f(u)\right](x).
 \label{eq:fractional-volterra-semigroup}
\end{equation}
For \(v\in E\), completeness of \(E\), and a strict endpoint \(a<x\), the same
beta calculation gives the normalized shifted-power rule
\begin{equation}
 I_{a+}^{\alpha}\!\left[t\mapsto
   \frac{(t-a)^{\beta-1}}{\Gamma(\beta)}v\right](x)
 =\frac{(x-a)^{\alpha+\beta-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}v
 \qquad(\alpha,\beta>0),
 \label{eq:fractional-volterra-normalized-rpow}
\end{equation}
and, for \(\rho>-1\),
\begin{equation}
 I_{a+}^{\alpha}\!\left[t\mapsto(t-a)^\rho v\right](x)
 =\frac{\Gamma(\rho+1)(x-a)^{\alpha+\rho}}
        {\Gamma(\alpha+\rho+1)}v
 \qquad(\alpha>0).
 \label{eq:fractional-volterra-rpow}
\end{equation}
The constant specialization includes the degenerate endpoint:
\begin{equation}
 I_{a+}^{\alpha}[t\mapsto v](x)
 =\frac{(x-a)^\alpha}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}v
 \qquad(\alpha>0,\ a\le x).
 \label{eq:fractional-volterra-const}
\end{equation}
For arbitrary real \(\alpha\), \(0<c\), and \(a\le x\), increasing-affine covariance
requires no regularity, integrability, or completeness hypothesis:
\begin{align}
 I_{(ca+d)+}^{\alpha}f(cx+d)
 &=c^{\alpha}I_{a+}^{\alpha}[t\mapsto f(ct+d)](x),
                                                               \label{eq:fractional-volterra-affine}\\
 I_{a+}^{\alpha}[t\mapsto f(ct+d)](x)
 &=\left(c^\alpha\right)^{-1}I_{(ca+d)+}^{\alpha}f(cx+d).     \label{eq:fractional-volterra-comp-affine}
\end{align}
Finally, let \(E\) be complete, let \(0<\alpha\), \(a\le x\), and suppose that
\(g\) is continuous on \([a,x]\), has the right-sided interior derivative \(g'\),
and that \(g'\) is interval-integrable.  Gamma-normalized integration by parts gives
\begin{equation}
 I_{a+}^{\alpha+1}g'(x)
 =I_{a+}^{\alpha}g(x)
  -\frac{(x-a)^\alpha}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}g(a).                 \label{eq:fractional-volterra-add-one-deriv}
\end{equation}
This includes \(a=x\); when \(g(a)=0\), the boundary term vanishes.  Only the right
derivative on the open interval is required; no two-sided derivative hypothesis is used.
\end{theorem}

The definition and ten exact theorems in \path{FractionalVolterra.lean} are
\lean{fractionalVolterra}, \lean{fractionalVolterra_self},
\lean{fractionalVolterra_congr}, \lean{fractionalVolterra_smul},
\lean{intervalIntegrable_fractionalVolterra_kernel},
\lean{intervalIntegral_eq_integral_min_of_eq_zero},
\lean{fractionalVolterra_eq_intervalIntegral_min_of_eq_zero},
\lean{fractionalVolterra_add_input}, \lean{fractionalVolterra_one}, and
\lean{fractionalVolterra_nat_succ}, together with
\lean{intervalIntegral_fractionalVolterra_betaKernel}.  The six theorems in
\path{FractionalVolterraSemigroup.lean} are
\lean{intervalIntegrable_fractionalVolterra_betaKernel},
\lean{intervalIntegral_fractionalVolterra_normalizedBetaKernel},
\lean{fractionalVolterra_normalized_rpow_smul},
\lean{fractionalVolterra_rpow_smul}, \lean{fractionalVolterra_const}, and
\lean{fractionalVolterra_add}.  The four theorems in
\path{FractionalVolterraCalculus.lean} are
\lean{fractionalVolterra_affine}, \lean{fractionalVolterra_comp_affine},
\lean{fractionalVolterra_add_one_deriv}, and
\lean{fractionalVolterra_add_one_deriv_of_eq_zero}.  Thus these three reusable operator
modules have one definition and twenty public theorems.  They prove no classical
order-zero law, reversed-endpoint fractional interpretation, semigroup theorem for merely
interval-integrable inputs or noncomplete targets, Caputo or Riemann--Liouville derivative
operator, complex-order theorem, nonpositive-scale affine covariance, or hierarchy-specific
formula by themselves.  The positive-real Fabius-CDF specialization is recorded in
\Cref{sec:probability-laplace-bridge}; the following total definition and six specialization
theorems bring the four-module total to two definitions and twenty-six public theorems.

The signed Fabius dilation law now enters only at the specialization step.

\begin{theorem}[Causal Rvachev fractional primitives and exact first shifts]
\label{thm:fabius-fractional-volterra-shift}
For every real \(\beta,x\), define the total causal primitive
\begin{equation}
 U_\beta(x):=I_{(-1)+}^{\beta}\Up(x).
 \label{eq:rvachev-fractional-primitive}
\end{equation}
If \(0<\beta\) and \(-1\le x\), compact support gives the exact cutoff
\begin{equation}
 U_\beta(x)
 =\frac1{\Gamma(\beta)}
   \int_{-1}^{\min(x,1)}(x-t)^{\beta-1}\Up(t)\dd t.
 \label{eq:rvachev-fractional-primitive-min}
\end{equation}
For every \(n\in\N\) and every real \(x\),
\begin{equation}
 U_{n+1}(x)=J_{-1}^{n+1}\Up(x).
 \label{eq:rvachev-fractional-primitive-natural}
\end{equation}
If \(0<\alpha\), \(0<\beta\), and \(-1\le x\), then
\begin{equation}
 U_{\alpha+\beta}(x)=I_{(-1)+}^{\alpha}U_\beta(x).
 \label{eq:rvachev-fractional-primitive-semigroup}
\end{equation}
For \(0<\alpha\), the signed extension satisfies, for every \(x\ge0\),
\begin{equation}
 I_{0+}^{\alpha+1}\mathcal F(x)
 =2^\alpha I_{0+}^{\alpha}\mathcal F\!\left(\frac{x}{2}\right).
 \label{eq:fractional-global-Fabius-shift}
\end{equation}
The bounded function satisfies, for every \(x\in[0,1]\),
\begin{equation}
 I_{0+}^{\alpha+1}F(x)
 =2^\alpha I_{0+}^{\alpha}F\!\left(\frac{x}{2}\right).
 \label{eq:fractional-bounded-Fabius-shift}
\end{equation}
Finally the compactly supported fold, integrated from its left support endpoint, obeys
for every \(x\ge-1\)
\begin{equation}
 I_{(-1)+}^{\alpha+1}\Up(x)
 =2^\alpha I_{0+}^{\alpha}F\!\left(\frac{x+1}{2}\right).
 \label{eq:fractional-rvachev-Up-shift}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

The total definition and six theorems in \path{FabiusFractionalVolterra.lean} are
\lean{rvachevFractionalPrimitive},
\lean{rvachevFractionalPrimitive_eq_intervalIntegral_min},
\lean{rvachevFractionalPrimitive_nat_succ},
\lean{rvachevFractionalPrimitive_add},
\lean{fractionalVolterra_add_one_extendedFabius_of_nonneg},
\lean{fractionalVolterra_add_one_fabiusReal}, and
\lean{fractionalVolterra_add_one_rvachevUp}.  Their domains are part of the statements:
the signed formula does not cover \(x<0\), the bounded formula is confined to
\([0,1]\), the cutoff and semigroup formulas start at \(-1\), and the support-truncated
formula is asserted only at positive order.

\begin{theorem}[Complete integer primitive ladder of the Fabius functions]
\label{thm:fabius-volterra-ladder}
For every \(n,p\in\N\) and \(x\in\R\),
\begin{align}
 J_0^n\mathcal F(x)
 &=2^{\binom n2}\mathcal F\!\left(\frac{x}{2^n}\right),     \label{eq:J-global-Fabius}\\
 J_0^n(t^p\mathcal F(t))(x)
 &=\sum_{r=0}^{p}(-1)^r\binom pr n^{\overline r}x^{p-r}
   2^{\binom{n+r}{2}}
   \mathcal F\!\left(\frac{x}{2^{n+r}}\right).              \label{eq:J-weighted-global-Fabius}
\end{align}
For the bounded function the same formulas hold whenever \(x\le1\):
\begin{align}
 J_0^nF(x)
 &=2^{\binom n2}F\!\left(\frac{x}{2^n}\right),              \label{eq:J-bounded-Fabius}\\
 J_0^n(t^pF(t))(x)
 &=\sum_{r=0}^{p}(-1)^r\binom pr n^{\overline r}x^{p-r}
   2^{\binom{n+r}{2}}
   F\!\left(\frac{x}{2^{n+r}}\right).                       \label{eq:J-weighted-bounded-Fabius}
\end{align}
All four identities include \(n=0\), arbitrary endpoint orientation, and the natural
power convention \(0^0=1\).
\end{theorem}

The four displayed statements are exactly
\lean{normalizedVolterra_extendedFabius},
\lean{normalizedVolterra_pow_mul_extendedFabius},
\lean{normalizedVolterra_fabiusReal_of_le_one}, and
\lean{normalizedVolterra_pow_mul_fabiusReal_of_le_one} in
\path{FabiusAntiderivatives.lean}.  Their proof architecture is deliberately triangular:
the global dilation equation identifies the \(n\)-th derivative of the scaled candidate;
its initial Taylor jet vanishes; \eqref{eq:J-taylor-remainder} reconstructs the candidate;
and \eqref{eq:J-monomial} supplies every natural polynomial weight.  The bounded formulas
use \lean{fabiusReal_eq_extendedFabius_of_le_one}: the oriented segment from \(0\) to any
\(x\le1\) remains inside the half-line on which the two functions agree.

\begin{boxedremark}
The generic positive-real operator is formalized in
\Cref{thm:fractional-volterra-foundation}, including its positive-real, ordered-endpoint,
continuous-Banach-input semigroup, shifted-power and constant evaluations,
positive-scale affine covariance, and right-derivative order-raising identity.  The
positive-real Fabius-CDF stopped-power and unit-endpoint moment formulas are formalized in
\Cref{thm:fabius-fractional-stopped-power}, and the first signed, bounded, and
\(\Up\)-to-\(F\) fractional shifts in
\Cref{thm:fabius-fractional-volterra-shift}.  What remains includes the signed branch for
\(x<0\), iterated fractional shifts, the shifted dyadic lattice and its endpoint-\(\Up\)
wrapper, reflected or piecewise support-tail formulas, weighted hierarchy-specific
monomials, semigroups for shifted or reflected hierarchy variants, a full two-branch refinement law,
fractional inverse/quantile formulas, classical order-zero or reversed-endpoint
interpretations, Caputo and Riemann--Liouville derivative operators, complex powers and
complex order, and normal or Mellin continuation.  At integer order, separately named
packages for the complete \(\Up\) ladder based at \(-1\), the reflected \(1-F\) ladder,
arbitrary affine copies of \(F\), and the full piecewise polynomial tail after the support
of \(\Up\) also remain frontier targets.
\end{boxedremark}

\subsection{Dyadic derivative cells and exact value distributions}
\label{sec:rvachev-derivative-distribution}

Write
\[
 T_n:=\binom{n+1}{2},
 \qquad
 \varepsilon_k:=(-1)^{\wt(k)}.
\]
For \(n,k\in\N\), the formal cell coordinate is the total function
\begin{equation}
 c_{n,k}(y):=-1+\frac{2k+1+y}{2^n}.                       \label{eq:rvachev-derivative-cell}
\end{equation}
Its geometric use is \(0\le k<2^n\) and \(-1\le y\le1\).  In that range it maps
\([-1,1]\) into \([-1,1]\), and
\begin{equation}
 c_{n,0}(-1)=-1,\qquad
 c_{n,k}(1)=c_{n,k+1}(-1),\qquad
 c_{n,2^n}(-1)=1.                                        \label{eq:rvachev-cell-endpoints}
\end{equation}
Thus the images are exactly the \(2^n\) adjacent closed cells from \(-1\) to \(1\).

\begin{theorem}[Exact cellwise derivative tiling]
\label{thm:rvachev-derivative-cell}
For every \(n\in\N\), \(0\le k<2^n\), and \(-1\le y\le1\),
\begin{align}
 \Up^{(n)}\!\left(c_{n,k}(y)\right)
 &=2^{T_n}\varepsilon_k\Up(y),                            \label{eq:rvachev-cell-signed}\\
 \left|\Up^{(n)}\!\left(c_{n,k}(y)\right)\right|
 &=2^{T_n}\Up(y).                                         \label{eq:rvachev-cell-absolute}
\end{align}
