 +P(\log_2 s)+E(s)
 \;}\qquad(s>0).                                            \label{eq:Lambda-exact-periodic}
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}
 0\le E(s)\le
 \frac{\exp(-s)}{(1-\exp(-s))^2},\qquad
 E(s)\le4\exp(-s)\quad(s\ge L).                             \label{eq:negative-Laplace-tail-bound}
\end{equation}
These are exactly
\lean{negativeLaplaceLog_exact_periodic_decomposition},
\lean{negativeLaplaceTailError_nonneg},
\lean{abs_negativeLaplaceTailError_le}, and
\lean{abs_negativeLaplaceTailError_le_four_exp} in
\path{NegativeLaplace.lean}.  The correction \(P\) is \(C^\infty\) and one-periodic by
\lean{contDiff_infty_negativeLaplacePeriodicCorrection} and
\lean{negativeLaplacePeriodicCorrection_add_one}.

Write
\begin{equation}
 \overline P=\int_0^1P(u)\,\dd u,\qquad
 \Psi(t)=P(t)-\overline P.                                  \label{eq:Psi-def}
\end{equation}
Then \(\Psi\) is \(C^\infty\), one-periodic, and has mean zero:
\[
 \Psi(t+1)=\Psi(t),\qquad \int_0^1\Psi(t)\,\dd t=0.
\]
The formal counterparts are
\lean{contDiff_infty_negativeLaplacePsi},
\lean{negativeLaplacePsi_add_one}, and
\lean{integral_negativeLaplacePsi_zero}.

The first logarithmic derivative has a correspondingly sharp full-ray expansion.  As
\(s\to+\infty\),
\begin{equation}
 \Lambda'(s)=
 \frac{-\log s/L+1/2+\Psi'(\log_2s)/L}{s}
 +\bigO(s^{-2}).                                            \label{eq:negative-Laplace-first-derivative}
\end{equation}
Here \(\Lambda'\) is the formal \lean{negativeLaplaceLogFirst}, identified with the
derivative of \(\Lambda\) by \lean{negativeLaplaceLog_hasDerivAt}.  The exact
\(\bigO(s^{-2})\) statement is
\lean{negativeLaplaceLogFirst_sub_periodic_main_isBigO_inv_sq_real} in
\path{LaplacePeriodicSecondOrder.lean}.  This promotes the proved derivative estimate
without importing the longer unformalized contour and Mellin derivations from the frontier
volume.

\subsection{The Gamma--zeta Fourier series}

For \(k\in\Z\), use the Fourier convention
\begin{equation}
 \widehat\Psi(k)=\int_0^1\Psi(t)\exp(-2\pi\ii kt)\,\dd t,
 \qquad
 \chi_k=\frac{2\pi\ii k}{L}.                                \label{eq:Psi-Fourier-def}
\end{equation}
The zero mode vanishes, and every nonzero mode has the closed form
\begin{equation}
 \widehat\Psi(0)=0,\qquad
 \widehat\Psi(k)=
 -\frac{\Gamma(-\chi_k)\zeta(1-\chi_k)}{L}
 \quad(k\ne0).                                              \label{eq:Psi-Fourier}
\end{equation}
Every coefficient with \(k\ne0\) is nonzero, so \(\Psi\) is genuinely nonconstant.
The coefficient sequence is absolutely summable and reconstructs \(\Psi\) pointwise:
\begin{equation}
 \Psi(t)=\sum_{k\in\Z}\widehat\Psi(k)\exp(2\pi\ii kt).
                                                                  \label{eq:Psi-Fourier-series}
\end{equation}
These assertions are
\lean{negativeLaplacePsiFourierCoeff_zero},
\lean{negativeLaplacePsiFourierCoeff_eq_neg_gamma_zeta},
\lean{negativeLaplacePsiFourierCoeff_ne_zero},
\lean{negativeLaplacePsi_not_constant},
\lean{summable_negativeLaplacePsiGammaZetaFourierCoeff}, and
\lean{hasSum_negativeLaplacePsi_gammaZeta_fourierSeries} in
\path{PeriodicFourier.lean}.  No numerical truncation or stronger coefficient-decay
claim is needed here.

\subsection{The evaluated constant}

Let \(\gamma\) be Euler's constant and let \(\gamma_1\) denote the formally defined first
Stieltjes constant \lean{firstStieltjesConstant}.  The regularized Gamma--zeta
combination
\[
 A_0=\frac{\gamma^2}{2}+\gamma_1-\frac{\pi^2}{12}
\]
is \lean{gammaZetaConstant}; its equivalent common-denominator form is proved by
\lean{gammaZetaConstant_eq_div_twelve}.  The split, convergent finite-part Bose integral
is evaluated as this same constant by
\lean{boseFinitePartIntegral_eq_gammaZetaConstant}.

The formal finite part is independent of where the convergent integral is split.  More
precisely, put
\[
 \kappa_0(x)=\frac1x\log\!\left(\frac{1-\exp(-x)}x\right),
 \qquad
 \kappa_\infty(x)=\frac1x\log(1-\exp(-x)).
\]
For every \(c>0\), both integrals below are convergent and
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 \int_{(0,c]}\kappa_0(x)\,dx+
 \int_{(c,\infty)}\kappa_\infty(x)\,dx+
 \frac{(\log c)^2}{2}=A_0.}                                  \label{eq:bose-finite-part-cutoff}
\end{equation}
If \(0<a\le b\), changing the splitting point is governed by the exact transition identity
\begin{equation}
 \int_{(a,b]}\bigl(\kappa_\infty(x)-\kappa_0(x)\bigr)\,dx
 =\frac{(\log b)^2-(\log a)^2}{2}.                           \label{eq:bose-cutoff-transition}
\end{equation}
Here the two kernels are \lean{boseFinitePartSmallKernel} and
\lean{boseFinitePartLargeKernel}; their integrability is
\lean{integrableOn_boseFinitePartSmallKernel_Ioc} and
\lean{integrableOn_boseFinitePartLargeKernel_Ioi_of_pos}.  Equations
\eqref{eq:bose-finite-part-cutoff} and \eqref{eq:bose-cutoff-transition} are respectively
\lean{boseFinitePartIntegral_eq_gammaZetaConstant_of_pos} and
\lean{integral_boseFinitePartLargeKernel_sub_small_Ioc} in
\path{BoseFinitePartIntegral.lean}.  Thus the constant is represented by ordinary convergent
real integrals, without assigning a value to a divergent integral.

In turn,
\lean{negativeLaplacePeriodicMean_eq} gives
\[
 \overline P=\frac{A_0}{L}-\frac{L}{12}.
\]
After the Gaussian normalization, the sharp Fabius constant is
\begin{equation}
 C_F=
 \frac{6\gamma^2+12\gamma_1-\pi^2}{12L}
 -\frac{7L}{12}-\frac12\log\pi.                             \label{eq:sharp-constant}
\end{equation}
This is the exact identity
\lean{fabiusSharpAsymptoticConstant_eq} in
\path{FabiusSharpConstant.lean}.

\subsection{A fully effective dyadic bound}

The dyadic specialization admits a proved numerical threshold and error constant.  For
\(n\ge1\), define
\begin{align}
 D_n={}&-\frac L2n^2-n\log n+\left(1+\frac L2\right)n
       -\frac{(\log n)^2}{2L}+C_F+\Psi(\log_2n) \notag\\
     &-\frac n2\bigl(\Lambda''(n)+\Lambda'(n)^2\bigr).       \label{eq:dyadic-effective-main}
\end{align}
Here the final term is exactly \lean{dyadicEndpointSecondOrder}; the derivative
identifications are \lean{negativeLaplaceLog_hasDerivAt} and
\lean{negativeLaplaceLogFirst_hasDerivAt}.  Then, for every \(n\ge224043\),
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 \left|\log F(2^{-n})-D_n\right|
 \le\frac{2512945}{12n}.}
                                                               \label{eq:dyadic-effective-bound}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{abs_log_fabius_dyadic_sub_explicitCumulantMain_le} in
\path{FabiusDyadicSharpCumulant.lean}.  The threshold is a sufficient certified threshold,
not an optimality claim.  Its proof uses the pointwise bounds
\lean{dyadicEndpointSecondOrder_sq_le},
\lean{dyadicHigherLaplaceMoments_le}, and
\lean{abs_dyadicEndpointLaplaceLogError_add_secondOrder_le_unconditional} in
\path{LaplaceMomentBounds.lean}, with respective constants \(256/n\), \(104448/n\), and
\(209408/n\) on their stated ranges.

\subsection{The lower-Lambert saddle and exact reduction}
\label{sec:sharp-asymptotic}

On the endpoint-inclusive lower-branch domain
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal D_L=\left(0,\frac{\e^{-1}}L\right],                \label{eq:lambert-phase-domain}
\end{equation}
let
\begin{equation}
 W=W_{-1}(-Lx),                                             \label{eq:W-def}
\end{equation}
and define
\begin{equation}
 \lambda=\lambda(x)=-\frac{W}{L},
 \qquad r=r(x)=2^\lambda.                                  \label{eq:lambda-def}
\end{equation}
The global Lean definitions are \lean{fabiusLambertPhase} and
\lean{fabiusLambertRadius}; here we use them on \(\mathcal D_L\).  For every
\(x\in\mathcal D_L\), their exact identities are
\begin{equation}
 \lambda2^{-\lambda}=x,\qquad
 \frac{\lambda}{r}=x,\qquad
 rx=\lambda,\qquad
 \log r=L\lambda.                                          \label{eq:lambda-equation}
\end{equation}
The endpoint-inclusive quotient and product identities are
\lean{fabiusLambertPhase_div_radius_of_le} and
\lean{fabiusLambertRadius_mul_argument_of_le}; the first equality in
\eqref{eq:lambda-equation} is the quotient identity rewritten using \(r=2^\lambda\).
The logarithmic identity in fact holds for every real \(x\), not only on
\(\mathcal D_L\); this is \lean{log_fabiusLambertRadius}.  The corresponding
strict-interior wrappers are \lean{fabiusLambertPhase_div_radius} and
\lean{fabiusLambertRadius_mul_argument}.  All five declarations are in
\path{FabiusLambertSaddle.lean}.

On the same closed-upper domain, the phase has the sharp lower bound and endpoint
characterization
\begin{equation}
 \lambda(x)\ge\frac1L,
 \qquad
 \lambda(x)=\frac1L
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 x=\frac{\e^{-1}}L.                                        \label{eq:lambert-phase-closed-bound}
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}
 \lambda\!\left(\frac{\e^{-1}}L\right)=\frac1L.             \label{eq:lambert-phase-branch-point}
\end{equation}
Moreover, \(\lambda\) is strictly decreasing on \(\mathcal D_L\), and its exact image is
\begin{equation}
 \lambda(\mathcal D_L)=\left[\frac1L,\infty\right).         \label{eq:lambert-phase-closed-image}
\end{equation}
The bound, equality characterization, endpoint value, strict antitonicity, and image are
respectively \lean{fabiusLambertPhase_ge_inv_log_two},
\lean{fabiusLambertPhase_eq_inv_log_two_iff},
\lean{fabiusLambertPhase_branchPoint},
\lean{fabiusLambertPhase_strictAntiOn_Ioc}, and
\lean{fabiusLambertPhase_image_Ioc} in \path{FabiusLambertSaddle.lean}.

Equivalently, under the hypotheses \(x>0\) and \(Lx\le\e^{-1}\), the same
lower bound and the strict positivity \(0<\lambda(x)\) are
\lean{one_div_log_two_le_fabiusLambertPhase} and
\lean{fabiusLambertPhase_pos_of_le}.  Thus the algebraic saddle identities
extend to the finite branch point even though the smooth formulas below do not.

The smooth API is deliberately restricted to the open interior
\(0<x<\e^{-1}/L\).  There \(\lambda\) is continuous and strictly decreasing, its image is
\((1/L,\infty)\), and it is differentiable with
\begin{equation}
 \lambda'(x)=
 \frac{\lambda(x)}{x\bigl(1-L\lambda(x)\bigr)}<0.            \label{eq:lambert-phase-derivative}
\end{equation}
These are \lean{fabiusLambertPhase_continuousOn},
\lean{fabiusLambertPhase_strictAntiOn}, \lean{fabiusLambertPhase_image},
\lean{deriv_fabiusLambertPhase}, and \lean{deriv_fabiusLambertPhase_neg} in
\path{FabiusLambertSaddle.lean}; the derivative witness is
\lean{fabiusLambertPhase_hasDerivAt}, and pointwise continuity is
\lean{fabiusLambertPhase_continuousAt}.
At the small-argument endpoint \(x=0\), the exact phase and its reciprocal have the limits
\begin{equation}
 \lambda(x)\longrightarrow+\infty,
 \qquad \lambda(x)^{-1}\longrightarrow0
 \qquad(x\downarrow0).                                    \label{eq:lambert-phase-endpoint-limits}
\end{equation}
These are \lean{tendsto_fabiusLambertPhase_nhdsGT_zero_atTop} and
\lean{tendsto_inv_fabiusLambertPhase_nhdsGT_zero} in
\path{FabiusLambertRates.lean}.

The corrected main term is
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal M(x)=
 -\frac L2\lambda(x)^2+
 \left(1+\frac L2\right)\lambda(x)
 -\frac12\log\lambda(x)+C_F+\Psi(\lambda(x)).
                                                               \label{eq:sharp-main-lambda}
\end{equation}
This is definition \lean{fabiusSharpLambertMain}.  The exact saddle action
itself is endpoint-inclusive: whenever \(x>0\) and \(Lx\le\e^{-1}\),
\begin{equation}
 r(x)x+\Lambda(r(x))-\frac12\log\bigl(2\pi\lambda(x)\bigr)
 =\mathcal M(x)+E(r(x)).                                  \label{eq:lambert-saddle-action}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{fabiusLambertSaddleAction_eq_of_le} in
\path{FabiusSharpLambertMain.lean}.  If
\(\mathcal R(x)\) denotes the normalized saddle ratio
\lean{fabiusSaddleRatio} at \(r(x)\) and \(\lambda(x)\), then Lean proves the exact
error separation
\begin{equation}
 \log F(x)-\mathcal M(x)=\log\mathcal R(x)+E(r(x)).
                                                               \label{eq:sharp-exact-reduction}
\end{equation}
The declaration is
\lean{log_fabius_sub_sharpLambertMain_eq_log_ratio_add_tail} in
\path{FabiusSharpExactReduction.lean}.

The forward product tail in this separation is negligible at every algebraic order of the
exact phase.  Along \(x=2^{-t}\), for each \(N\in\N\),
\begin{equation}
 E\!\left(r(2^{-t})\right)
 =o\!\left(\lambda(2^{-t})^{-N}\right)
 \qquad(t\to+\infty).                                     \label{eq:lambert-tail-all-power-little-o}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{negativeLaplaceTailError_dyadicLambert_isLittleO_inv_pow} in
\path{FabiusSharpLambertTransfer.lean}.  At \(N=0\) it says directly that the tail tends
to zero; larger \(N\) show that it contributes to none of the inverse-phase coefficients.

The analytic input to that reduction is an exact Bromwich formula.  For every \(r>0\)
and \(x\in\R\), as an identity in \(\C\),
\begin{equation}
 F(x)=\exp(rx)\int_{\R}
 \exp(2\pi\ii\xi x)\,
 \frac{G(-(r+2\pi\ii\xi))}{r+2\pi\ii\xi}\,\dd\xi.
                                                               \label{eq:bromwich}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{fabius_bromwich}; the dimensionless, Gaussian-normalized version used by
the saddle machinery is \lean{fabius_bromwich_scaled}.  The supporting Laplace-transform
identity is \lean{generatingFunction_neg_div_eq_laplace}.  All three occur in
\path{LaplaceTransform.lean} and \path{FabiusBromwichInput.lean} with the positivity and
integrability hypotheses shown there.

\subsection{The compact online formula and its missing periodic factor}
\label{sec:compact-online-lambert}

A January 2022 comment in a Mathematics Stack Exchange discussion asked whether the
preceding calculation implied a particularly compact lower-Lambert formula for the
endpoint behavior \cite{reshetnikovMSEasymptotic}.  With the notation of
\eqref{eq:W-def}, its logarithmic main term is
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal W_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x)=
 -\frac{7L}{12}-\frac12\log(\pi x)
 +\frac1L\left(
   \gamma_1+\frac{\gamma^2}{2}-\frac{\pi^2}{12}
   -W-\frac{W^2}{2}
 \right).
                                                               \label{eq:mse-lambert-log-main}
\end{equation}
This is the human-readable form of definition \lean{fabiusWikipediaLambertMain} in
\path{FabiusWikipediaMain.lean}.  Written in ordinary mathematical notation, the
exponential of this logarithmic encoding is the expression printed in the discussion:
\begin{equation}
 A_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x)=
 \frac{1}{2^{7/12}\sqrt{\pi x}}
 \exp\!\left[
  \frac1L\left(
   \gamma_1+\frac{\gamma^2}{2}-\frac{\pi^2}{12}
   -W-\frac{W^2}{2}
  \right)
 \right].                                                     \label{eq:mse-lambert-main}
\end{equation}
For \(x>0\), this factor is strictly positive.  Lean names it
\lean{fabiusWikipediaLambertFactor};
\lean{exp_fabiusWikipediaLambertMain_eq_WikipediaLambertFactor} identifies it
with \(\exp(\mathcal W_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x))\), and
\lean{fabiusWikipediaLambertFactor_pos} proves its positivity.  All three
declarations occur in \path{FabiusWikipediaMain.lean}.
The proposed symbol \(F(x)\sim A_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x)\) means that the ratio should tend
to \(1\) as \(x\downarrow0\).

That ratio-one claim is false.  The answer on the cited page first derives a Gamma--zeta
periodic term, then explicitly omits it from the subsequent approximation while noting
that it must be retained for more precise asymptotics.  The exact formal decomposition
shows what is lost: on the positive lower-Lambert domain,
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal M(x)=\mathcal W_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x)+\Psi(\lambda(x)).
                                                               \label{eq:mse-corrected-log-main}
\end{equation}
Here \lean{fabiusCorrectedWikipediaMain_eq_WikipediaLambertMain_add} is the all-real
identity between the two named compact definitions, while
\lean{fabiusCorrectedWikipediaMain_eq_sharpLambertMain} identifies the corrected one
with \(\mathcal M\) on the stated domain.  Consequently the proved ratio-one asymptotic
is
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\begin{aligned}
 F(x)\sim{}&
 \frac{1}{2^{7/12}\sqrt{\pi x}}
 \exp\!\left[
  \frac1L\left(
   \gamma_1+\frac{\gamma^2}{2}-\frac{\pi^2}{12}
   -W-\frac{W^2}{2}
  \right)
 \right]\\
 &{}\times\exp\!\left(\Psi(-W/L)\right)
 \end{aligned}}
 \qquad(x\downarrow0).                                      \label{eq:mse-lambert-corrected}
\end{equation}
This is the direct theorem
\lean{fabius_isEquivalent_WikipediaLambertFactor_mul_periodic} in
\path{FabiusSharpAsymptotic.lean}; the underlying exact positive-side factorization is
\lean{exp_fabiusCorrectedWikipediaMain_eq_WikipediaLambertFactor_mul_periodic}
in \path{FabiusWikipediaMain.lean}.  The plus sign in
\eqref{eq:mse-corrected-log-main} is essential: the missing multiplier is
\(\exp(\Psi(\lambda(x)))\), with \(\lambda(x)=-W/L\).

The logical bridge from logarithmic error to ratio-one equivalence is itself formalized.
Along any filter, if \(f\) is eventually positive, then
\begin{equation}
 f\sim\exp(g)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \log f-g\longrightarrow0.
                                                               \label{eq:exp-equivalent-log-error}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{isEquivalent_exp_iff_tendsto_log_sub}.  In the present case Lean proves
both
\begin{equation}
 \log F(x)-\mathcal W_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x)\not\longrightarrow0
 \qquad(x\downarrow0)                                       \label{eq:mse-log-error-nonzero}
\end{equation}
and that the same residual is not
\(\bigO(({-\log x})^{-1})\).  These are respectively
\lean{log_fabius_sub_WikipediaLambertMain_not_tendsto_zero} and
\lean{log_fabius_sub_WikipediaLambertMain_not_isBigO}.  Applying
\eqref{eq:exp-equivalent-log-error} gives the direct theorem
\begin{equation}
 F\not\sim A_{\mathrm{MSE}},                                 \label{eq:mse-not-equivalent}
\end{equation}
namely \lean{fabius_not_isEquivalent_exp_WikipediaLambertMain}; its direct
literal-factor form is \lean{fabius_not_isEquivalent_WikipediaLambertFactor}.
The criterion and all four obstruction declarations in this paragraph occur in
\path{FabiusSharpAsymptotic.lean}.  Thus the nonconvergence of the sampled periodic
correction, rather than its numerical size, is decisive for ratio-one equivalence.

\begin{theorem}[Exact cluster interval for the online quotient]
\label{thm:mse-lambert-ratio-cluster}
For \(x>0\), put
\[
 R_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x):=\frac{F(x)}{A_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x)},\qquad
 m_\Psi:=\min_{0\le t\le1}\Psi(t),\qquad
 M_\Psi:=\max_{0\le t\le1}\Psi(t).
\]
Then \(m_\Psi<M_\Psi\), and the quotient has the sharper additive asymptotic model
\begin{equation}
 R_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x)-\exp\!\bigl(\Psi(\lambda(x))\bigr)\longrightarrow0
 \qquad(x\downarrow0).                                      \label{eq:mse-lambert-ratio-model}
\end{equation}
Write \(\operatorname{Cl}_{0^+}(R_{\mathrm{MSE}})\) for the set of all \(z\in\R\)
for which there is a sequence \(x_n\to0\), eventually positive, such that
\(R_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x_n)\to z\).  Its complete cluster set is
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{Cl}_{0^+}(R_{\mathrm{MSE}})
   =[\exp(m_\Psi),\exp(M_\Psi)].                            \label{eq:mse-lambert-cluster-interval}
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}
 \liminf_{x\downarrow0}R_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x)=\exp(m_\Psi)
 <\exp(M_\Psi)=\limsup_{x\downarrow0}R_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x).  \label{eq:mse-lambert-liminf-limsup}
\end{equation}
Consequently no real constant repairs the omitted factor: in Mathlib's
filter-theoretic asymptotic-equivalence relation,
\begin{equation}
 \forall c\in\R,\qquad
 F\not\sim cA_{\mathrm{MSE}}\quad(x\downarrow0).             \label{eq:mse-lambert-no-constant}
\end{equation}
For \(c\ne0\), the last assertion is the usual failure of
\(F(x)/(cA_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x))\to1\).  The formal declaration also includes
\(c=0\), without interpreting a quotient by zero.
\end{theorem}

The online discussion supplies \(A_{\mathrm{MSE}}\); the exact cluster interval is a
consequence of the corrected formal asymptotic.  The endpoint descriptions
\(\exp(m_\Psi)\) and \(\exp(M_\Psi)\) are exact variational formulas in terms of the
explicit periodic correction.  No finite elementary evaluation, numerical certification,
sign claim for the extrema, or uniqueness claim for their phases is asserted here.

The phase-sampling mechanism is reusable.  Every value of a one-periodic map \(p\) is a
cluster value of \(p(\lambda(x))\) as \(x\downarrow0\); if \(p\) is continuous and its
codomain is Hausdorff, the complete cluster set is exactly \(p(\R)\).  These statements
are \lean{mapClusterPt_periodic_comp_fabiusLambertPhase} and
\lean{mapClusterPt_periodic_comp_fabiusLambertPhase_iff} in
\path{FabiusWikipediaObstruction.lean}.

For the specialization above, \(R_{\mathrm{MSE}}\) is
\lean{fabiusWikipediaLambertRatio}, while \(m_\Psi\) and \(M_\Psi\) are
\lean{negativeLaplacePsiPeriodMin} and \lean{negativeLaplacePsiPeriodMax}.  The exact
global range, pointwise extremal bounds, and endpoint order are respectively
\lean{range_negativeLaplacePsi_eq_Icc_periodMin_periodMax},
\lean{negativeLaplacePsiPeriodMin_le_value},
\lean{negativeLaplacePsi_value_le_periodMax}, and
\lean{negativeLaplacePsiPeriodMin_le_periodMax}; attainment and strict separation are
\lean{exists_mem_Icc_negativeLaplacePsi_eq_periodMin},
\lean{exists_mem_Icc_negativeLaplacePsi_eq_periodMax}, and
\lean{negativeLaplacePsiPeriodMin_lt_periodMax}.  Exponentiation of the exact range is
\lean{range_exp_negativeLaplacePsi_eq_Icc_periodExtrema}.

The ratio-one comparison with the sampled periodic model is
\lean{fabiusWikipediaLambertRatio_isEquivalent_periodic}, and the stronger additive
statement \eqref{eq:mse-lambert-ratio-model} is
\lean{tendsto_fabiusWikipediaLambertRatio_sub_periodic}.  The interval identity
\eqref{eq:mse-lambert-cluster-interval} is
\lean{mapClusterPt_fabiusWikipediaLambertRatio_nhdsGT_zero_iff}; the two endpoint limits
are \lean{liminf_fabiusWikipediaLambertRatio_nhdsGT_zero_eq} and
\lean{limsup_fabiusWikipediaLambertRatio_nhdsGT_zero_eq}; and the universal obstruction
is \lean{fabius_not_isEquivalent_const_mul_WikipediaLambertFactor}.  These declarations
all occur in \path{FabiusSharpAsymptotic.lean}.

\subsection{The first two periodic saddle corrections}

Define
\begin{equation}
 A_1(u)=
 \frac1{24}+\frac1{2L}
 -\frac{\Psi''(u)+\Psi'(u)^2}{2L^2}.                       \label{eq:A1}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{fabiusFirstSaddleCorrection}.  It is continuous, one-periodic, and bounded
by
\lean{continuous_fabiusFirstSaddleCorrection},
\lean{fabiusFirstSaddleCorrection_periodic}, and
\lean{isBounded_range_fabiusFirstSaddleCorrection}.  The first corrected expansion is
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\;
 \log F(x)=\mathcal M(x)+
 \frac{A_1(\lambda(x))}{\lambda(x)}
 +\bigO\!\left(\lambda(x)^{-2}\right)
 \;}\qquad(x\downarrow0).                                  \label{eq:first-corrected-asymptotic}
\end{equation}
It is the \(N=2\) specialization of
\lean{log_fabius_sub_sharpLambertExpansion_isBigO}, together with
\lean{fabiusSharpLambertExpansion_two}.

The second coefficient has a compact closed form in the bounded exponent jets.  For each
\(m\), write \(J_m(u)\) for the jet
\lean{negativeLaplaceBoundedExponentJet}\((m,u)\), and define
\begin{equation}
 \begin{split}
 A_2(u)=\frac1{24}\bigl(&8J_0^3+12J_0^2J_1+12J_0^2+48J_0J_1+12J_0J_2\\
                       &+6J_1^2+24J_1+20J_2+3J_3\bigr)(u).
 \end{split}                                                \label{eq:A2-jets}
\end{equation}
This is exactly \lean{fabiusSecondSaddleCorrection}; it is continuous, one-periodic,
and bounded by
\lean{continuous_fabiusSecondSaddleCorrection},
\lean{fabiusSecondSaddleCorrection_periodic}, and
\lean{isBounded_range_fabiusSecondSaddleCorrection}.  The formally proved
second-correction theorem is
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\;
 \log F(x)=\mathcal M(x)+
 \frac{A_1(\lambda(x))}{\lambda(x)}
 +\frac{A_2(\lambda(x))}{\lambda(x)^2}
 +\bigO\!\left(\lambda(x)^{-3}\right)
 \;}\qquad(x\downarrow0).                                  \label{eq:second-corrected-asymptotic}
\end{equation}
Its exact declarations are
\lean{fabiusSharpLambertExpansion_three} and
\lean{log_fabius_sub_twoSaddleCorrections_isBigO} in
\path{FabiusSecondSaddleExpansion.lean}; the companion theorem
\lean{log_fabius_sub_twoSaddleCorrections_isBigO_negLog} gives the literal remainder
\(\bigO((-\log x)^{-3})\).

\subsection{The recursive all-orders theorem}

For \(j\in\N\), let \(a_j(u)\) denote
\lean{fabiusSaddleLogCoefficient}\((j,u)\).
Every \(a_j\) is continuous, one-periodic, and bounded, by
\lean{continuous_fabiusSaddleLogCoefficient},
\lean{fabiusSaddleLogCoefficient_periodic}, and
\lean{isBounded_range_fabiusSaddleLogCoefficient}.  The initial identifications are
\[
 a_0=0,\qquad a_1=A_1,\qquad a_2=A_2,
\]
proved by
\lean{fabiusSaddleLogCoefficient_zero},
\lean{fabiusSaddleLogCoefficient_one_eq_firstSaddleCorrection}, and
\lean{fabiusSaddleLogCoefficient_two_eq_secondSaddleCorrection}.

For every \(N\in\N\), the complete exact-phase expansion is
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\;
 \log F(x)=\mathcal M(x)+
 \sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{a_j(\lambda(x))}{\lambda(x)^j}
 +\bigO\!\left(\lambda(x)^{-N}\right)
 \;}\qquad(x\downarrow0).                                  \label{eq:all-orders-log}
\end{equation}
The precise formal statement is
\lean{log_fabius_sub_sharpLambertMain_hasAsymptoticExpansion}; its finite-remainder
forms are
\lean{log_fabius_sub_sharpLambertExpansion_isBigO} and
\lean{log_fabius_sub_sharpLambertExpansion_isBigO_negLog} in
\path{FabiusFullAsymptoticExpansion.lean}.

The coefficients are constructive rather than existential.  The formal development
identifies the jet weights as coefficients of
\[
 \prod_{k=0}^{n-1}\bigl(X-(k+1)\bigr),
\]
proves closed forms for the periodic and bounded exponent jets, and gives recursive
exponent-polynomial identities.  The exact declarations are
\lean{negativeLaplaceJetPolynomial_eq_prod},
\lean{negativeLaplaceJetStirling_eq_coeff},
\lean{negativeLaplacePeriodicJet_eq_closedForm},
\lean{negativeLaplaceBoundedExponentJet_eq_closedForm},
\lean{negativeLaplaceExponentCoefficient_succ_eq}, and
\lean{negativeLaplaceExponentPolynomial_succ_eq}.  This coefficient-of-a-product
description is the proved statement; no identification with an external Stirling-number
API is asserted here.

The algebraic conversion between relative-mass and logarithmic coefficients is normalized
at every index rather than only asymptotically.  If \(\operatorname{ExpCoeff}\) and
\(\operatorname{LogCoeff}\) denote the formal recurrences \lean{expCoeff} and
\lean{logCoeff}, then
\begin{align}
 \operatorname{ExpCoeff}(\operatorname{LogCoeff}(a))_n
  &=\begin{cases}1,&n=0,\\a_n,&n>0,\end{cases} \notag\\
 \operatorname{LogCoeff}(\operatorname{ExpCoeff}(E))_n
  &=\begin{cases}0,&n=0,\\E_n,&n>0.\end{cases}              \label{eq:saddle-coefficient-normalization}
\end{align}
These are \lean{expCoeff_logCoeff_eq_ite} and
\lean{logCoeff_expCoeff_eq_ite}; the corresponding identities of formal power series are
\lean{expSeries_logCoeff_eq_massSeries_normalize_zero} and
\lean{logSeries_expCoeff_eq_exponentSeries_normalize_zero} in
\path{SaddleLogExpansionAlgebra.lean} and
\path{SaddleLogExpansionPowerSeries.lean}.

The recurrence-defined logarithm is also multiplicative at the level of
formal power series.  Let \(R\) be a commutative \(\Q\)-algebra and define
\[
 \mathfrak L(C):=\lean{SaddleExpansion.logOf}(C),
 \qquad C\in R[[X]],
\]
by applying \lean{logSeries} to the coefficient family of \(C\).  Rebuilding
a series from that family gives \(C\) itself by
\lean{SaddleExpansion.massSeries_coeff}; this reconstruction lemma needs only
that \(R\) be a commutative ring.  For every \(C\), even before a unit-constant
hypothesis is imposed,
\begin{equation}
 [X^0]\mathfrak L(C)=0,                                    \label{eq:formal-log-zero-constant}
\end{equation}
which is \lean{SaddleExpansion.constantCoeff_logOf}.  If
\([X^0]C=1\), then
\begin{equation}
 C\,\mathfrak L(C)'=C',                                   \label{eq:formal-log-differential-equation}
\end{equation}
by \lean{SaddleExpansion.mul_derivative_logOf}; conversely,
\lean{SaddleExpansion.logOf_eq_of} says that
\eqref{eq:formal-log-zero-constant} and
\eqref{eq:formal-log-differential-equation} characterize \(\mathfrak L(C)\)
uniquely.

It follows that unit-constant series satisfy
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak L(AB)=\mathfrak L(A)+\mathfrak L(B),
 \qquad \mathfrak L(1)=0,                                 \label{eq:formal-log-multiplicative}
\end{equation}
and, for every finite set \(S\) and family with \([X^0]F_i=1\),
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak L\!\left(\prod_{i\in S}F_i\right)
 =\sum_{i\in S}\mathfrak L(F_i).                         \label{eq:formal-log-finite-product}
\end{equation}
These are \lean{SaddleExpansion.logOf_mul},
\lean{SaddleExpansion.logOf_one}, and
\lean{SaddleExpansion.logOf_prod} in
\path{LogSeriesMultiplicative.lean}.  They are identities in \(R[[X]]\): no
convergence, analytic logarithm, branch choice, or passage to an infinite
product is asserted.

\subsection{Universal finite endpoint-transfer algebra}
\label{sec:endpoint-transfer-polynomials}

The formal coefficient stage of endpoint transfer is independent of every
probability and asymptotic estimate.  To distinguish the universal endpoint
polynomials from the periodic correction \(P\), write
\(P_r^{\mathrm{end}}(X)=\lean{endpointTransferPolynomial}(r)\).  In
\(\Q[X]\), define
\begin{equation}
 e_0(X)=0,\qquad e_{n+1}(X)=-\frac{X^{n+2}}{n+2},\qquad
 P_r^{\mathrm{end}}(X)=\operatorname{ExpCoeff}(e)_r.        \label{eq:endpoint-transfer-coefficients}
\end{equation}
Then the recursive, formal-series, and unordered-partition descriptions agree:
\begin{align}
 P_{n+1}^{\mathrm{end}}(X)
   &=-\frac1{n+1}\sum_{j=0}^{n}\frac{j+1}{j+2}
       X^{j+2}P_{n-j}^{\mathrm{end}}(X),                    \label{eq:endpoint-transfer-recurrence}\\
 \sum_{r\ge0}P_r^{\mathrm{end}}(X)t^r
   &=\exp\!\left(\sum_{r\ge0}e_r(X)t^r\right)
    =\exp\!\left(-\sum_{j\ge2}\frac{X^jt^{j-1}}j\right), \label{eq:endpoint-transfer-formal-series}\\
 P_r^{\mathrm{end}}(X)
   &=\sum_{f\in\mathcal P_r}\prod_{j=1}^{r}
       \frac{e_j(X)^{f(j)}}{f(j)!},                        \notag\\
 \mathcal P_r
   &=\left\{f:\N\to\N\;\middle|\;
       \substack{\supp(f)\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r\},\\
                 \sum_{j=1}^{r}j f(j)=r}\right\}.         \label{eq:endpoint-transfer-partitions}
\end{align}
The first coefficients are
\begin{equation}
 P_0^{\mathrm{end}}=1,\qquad P_1^{\mathrm{end}}=-\frac{X^2}{2},\qquad
 P_2^{\mathrm{end}}=\frac{X^4}{8}-\frac{X^3}{3},\qquad
 P_3^{\mathrm{end}}=-\frac{X^6}{48}+\frac{X^5}{6}-\frac{X^4}{4}. \label{eq:endpoint-transfer-first-four}
\end{equation}
Because \(\Q[X]\) is the free rational algebra on one generator, evaluating
\(X\) at any element \(z\) of a commutative \(\Q\)-algebra commutes both with
each \(P_r^{\mathrm{end}}\) and with the complete formal power series.

\begin{proof}[Formal proof]
The definitions are \lean{endpointTransferExponentCoefficient},
\lean{endpointTransferPolynomial}, and \lean{endpointTransferSeries} in
\path{EndpointTransferPolynomials.lean}.  Equations
\eqref{eq:endpoint-transfer-recurrence}--\eqref{eq:endpoint-transfer-partitions}
are respectively \lean{endpointTransferPolynomial_succ},
\lean{endpointTransferSeries_eq_exp_subst}, and
\lean{endpointTransferPolynomial_eq_partitionExpSum}.  The finite partition
engine and its equality with the recursive exponential coefficients are
\lean{weightedPartitions}, \lean{partitionExpSum}, and
\lean{partitionExpSum_eq_expCoeff} in \path{ExponentialPartition.lean} and
\path{ExponentialBell.lean}.  Evaluation and series naturality are
\lean{aeval_endpointTransferPolynomial} and
\lean{map_endpointTransferSeries}; the four identities in
\eqref{eq:endpoint-transfer-first-four} are
\lean{endpointTransferPolynomial_zero},
\lean{endpointTransferPolynomial_one},
\lean{endpointTransferPolynomial_two}, and
\lean{endpointTransferPolynomial_three}.

All statements in this subsection are finite or formal.  They do not identify
the series analytically with \(\exp(X)(1-Xt)^{1/t}\), construct a tilted law,
or prove a Taylor-window, tail, or endpoint-remainder estimate.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Recursive elementary re-expansion of the phase}
\label{sec:elementary-asymptotic}

The exact Lambert phase can itself be expanded to arbitrary order.  With
\[
 T(x)=-\log_2x,
\]
write \(\mathcal L_N(x)\) for
\lean{fabiusLambertLogarithmicApproximation}\((N,x)\).  This approximation is built from the
recursively defined polynomials
\lean{dyadicLambertDisplacementPolynomial}.  For every \(N\in\N\),
\begin{equation}
 \lambda(x)-\mathcal L_N(x)
 =\bigO\!\left(T(x)^{-N}\right)
 \qquad(x\downarrow0).                                     \label{eq:lambert-recursive-all-orders}
\end{equation}
This is
\lean{fabiusLambertPhase_sub_logarithmicApproximation_isBigO}; the equivalent dyadic-scale
form is \lean{dyadicLambertAllOrderRemainder_isBigO}.  Explicit coefficient polynomials
are proved through index two, while higher orders are represented by the formal recursion.

The periodic term cannot simply be discarded from a sharp estimate.  If
\(\mathcal M_{\mathrm{Wiki}}\) denotes the uncorrected elementary expression
\lean{fabiusWikipediaElementaryMain}, then Lean proves that
\(x\mapsto\log F(x)-\mathcal M_{\mathrm{Wiki}}(x)\) is not
\(\bigO((-\log x)^{-1})\) as \(x\downarrow0\).
This is \lean{log_fabius_sub_WikipediaElementaryMain_not_isBigO} in
\path{FabiusSharpAsymptotic.lean}.  The obstruction is therefore a theorem, not a
numerical diagnosis.

\subsection{Three endpoint consequences already formalized}

On the dyadic logarithmic scale, let
\(g(t)=-\log F(2^{-t})\).  Then
\begin{equation}
 \lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{g(t)}{t^2}=\frac{L}{2}.             \label{eq:dyadic-quadratic-law}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{fabiusLogProfile_normalized_tendsto} in
\path{FabiusLogSquaredAsymptotic.lean}.  On the original small-argument scale, for every
\(c>0\),
\begin{equation}
 \exp(-c/x)=o(F(x))\qquad(x\downarrow0).                     \label{eq:exp-inverse-little-o}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{exp_neg_div_isLittleO_fabius} in
\path{FabiusDecayComparison.lean}.  At the opposite endpoint, the exact global
symmetry already proved in \eqref{eq:shape-symmetry-global} gives only the identity
\begin{equation}
 1-F(1-x)=F(x).                                             \label{eq:right-endpoint-sym}
\end{equation}
No additional right-endpoint asymptotic-transfer theorem is asserted here.

\section{The Fourier--Legendre expansion of \(\Up\)}
\label{sec:legendre}

Let \(P_n\) be the Legendre polynomial normalized by \(P_n(1)=1\), and define
\begin{equation}
 u_n=\frac{4n+1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\Up(x)P_{2n}(x)\dd x.      \label{eq:un-legendre}
\end{equation}
Odd Legendre coefficients vanish by
\lean{rvachevFullLegendreCoefficient_odd_eq_zero}.  The even coefficients have the exact
finite formula
\begin{align}
 u_n={}&4^{-n}(4n+1)\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{n+k}
 \binom{2n}{n+k}\binom{2n+2k}{2n}                         \notag\\
 &\hspace{5em}\times(2k)!\,2^{\binom{2k+1}{2}}
 F(2^{-2k-1}).                                             \label{eq:legendre-exact-coeff}
\end{align}
This is \lean{rvachevLegendreCoefficient_eq_fabius_sum} in
\path{FabiusLegendreCoefficients.lean}.

The even series
\begin{equation}
 \Up(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}u_nP_{2n}(x)\qquad(-1\le x\le1) \label{eq:legendre-series}
\end{equation}
converges absolutely and uniformly in the formal development.  The exact declarations are
\lean{summable_abs_rvachevLegendreCoefficient},
\lean{hasSum_rvachevLegendreSeries}, and
\lean{hasSum_rvachevLegendreSeries_uniform} in
\path{FabiusLegendreSeries.lean}.  Its \(N\)-th polynomial partial sum minimizes squared
\(L^2([-1,1])\) error among all polynomials of degree at most \(2N+1\), and every distinct
competitor has strictly larger error.  These are
\lean{rvachevLegendrePartialSum_least_squares} and
\lean{rvachevLegendrePartialSum_strict_least_squares} in
\path{FabiusLegendreLeastSquares.lean}.  On $[0,2]$, the signed global extension has the
corresponding translated Legendre series
\lean{globalFabius_eq_tsum_translatedLegendre_formula}.

\section{Additional proved summation identities}
\label{sec:additional-identities}

On the support interval \(-1\le t\le1\), Poisson summation yields the exact cosine series
\begin{equation}
 \Up(t)=\frac12+
 \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}
 \widehat{\Up}\!\left(\frac{2k+1}{2}\right)
 \cos\bigl((2k+1)\pi t\bigr).                              \label{eq:cosine-series}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{rvachev_cosine_series}.  The two-term support specialization
\begin{equation}
 \Up(t)+\Up(t-1)=1\qquad(0\le t\le1)                       \label{eq:two-term-partition}
\end{equation}
is \lean{rvachev_add_shift_eq_one}.  More general scaled Poisson support identities are
sharp for either orientation of the lattice: whenever \(1/2\le|a|\),
\begin{align}
 1+2\Up(a)
   &=\sum_{m\in\Z}|a|^{-1}\widehat{\Up}(m/a),              \label{eq:poisson-support-abs-unscaled}\\
 |a|+2|a|\Up(a)
   &=\sum_{m\in\Z}\widehat{\Up}(m/a).                     \label{eq:poisson-support-abs}
\end{align}
These are \lean{rvachev_poisson_support_specialization_unscaled_of_one_half_le_abs} and
\lean{rvachev_poisson_support_specialization_of_one_half_le_abs} in
\path{PoissonSummation.lean}; the oriented frequency \(m/a\) is retained while the
covolume is \(|a|\).  They supplement the partition formulas
\eqref{eq:partition-unity}--\eqref{eq:integer-partition}; no unformalized derivative
partition, probabilistic convolution interpretation, or moment statistic is inferred.

\section{Effective computability}
\label{sec:computation}
\label{sec:effective-computability}

The centered spline construction gives a primitive-recursive rational approximation
procedure.  In the exact coding used by Lean, for every dyadic input code \(c\) and precision
\(p\),
\begin{equation}
 \left|
 F\!\left(c.\operatorname{value}(p+3)\right)
 -\operatorname{Approx}(c,p).\operatorname{value}(p)
 \right|
 \le 5\,2^{-(p+3)}.                                       \label{eq:computable-spline-error}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{fabiusSplineApproxPR_error}; the procedure itself is proved primitive
recursive by \lean{fabiusSplineApproxPR_primrec}.  Together with the sharp Lipschitz bound it
proves that every bounded Fabius solution is sequentially computable and effectively
uniformly continuous, hence a computable real function in the formal Grzegorczyk-style
definition.  The declarations are
\lean{fabiusReal_sequentiallyComputable},
\lean{fabiusReal_effectivelyUniformContinuous}, and
\lean{fabiusReal_isComputableRealFunction} in
\path{FabiusComputableSpline.lean} and \path{FabiusComputability.lean}.
The same effective continuity holds for every signed global extension:
\lean{extendedFabius_effectivelyUniformContinuous}.  For the canonical extension
\(\mathcal F=\lean{globalFabius}\), the explicit modulus is \(d(n)=2n\): whenever
\(n>0\) and \(|x-y|<1/(2n)\),
\begin{equation}
 |\mathcal F(x)-\mathcal F(y)|<\frac1n.                      \label{eq:global-effective-modulus}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{globalFabius_effectively_uniformly_continuous}, together with the definition
\lean{fabiusEffectiveUniformModulus}, in \path{FabiusComputability.lean}.

The same finite spline code also gives a signed-global algorithm.  Instead of clamping the
positive numerator to the unit grid, it retains the unrestricted centered spline and rounds
the signed rational numerator pair.  Negative dyadic inputs collapse to the zero grid point,
which is exact because \(
\mathcal F(x)=0\) for \(x\le0\).  The resulting primitive-recursive procedure satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \left|
 \mathcal F\!\left(c.\operatorname{value}(p+3)\right)
 -\operatorname{Approx}_{\rm glob}(c,p).\operatorname{value}(p)
 \right|
 \le 5\,2^{-(p+3)}.                                      \label{eq:computable-global-spline-error}
\end{equation}
These are \lean{extendedFabiusSplineApproxPR_primrec} and
\lean{extendedFabiusSplineApproxPR_error}.  Hence, for every \(F\) satisfying
\lean{IsFabius F}, its signed extension \lean{extendedFabius F} is a computable real function, by
\lean{extendedFabius_isComputableRealFunction}; the canonical specialization is
\lean{globalFabius_isComputableRealFunction}.  This is a primitive-recursive correctness
statement, not a practical running-time estimate: the unrestricted positive grid numerator
sets the length of the finite fold.

The quantitative analytic estimate is stable under moving the evaluation point:
\begin{equation}
 |S_p(x)-\mathcal F(y)|\le 2^{-p}+2|x-y|.                 \label{eq:computable-spline-moving-input}
\end{equation}
This is \lean{abs_fabiusUniformSpline_sub_extendedFabius_le_add}.  In particular, if
\(x_p\to x\), then \(S_p(x_p)\to\mathcal F(x)\), as packaged by
\lean{fabiusUniformSpline_tendsto_extendedFabius_of_tendsto}.

\section{Formalization boundary and audit conventions}
\label{sec:audit}

This exposition follows a fail-closed rule.  A mathematical assertion remains in the main
document only when an exact proved declaration can be identified in the Lean development.
A nearby theorem, an executable computation, a numerical check, or an argument that appears
straightforward is not treated as a substitute.  When the formal theorem has stricter
hypotheses, narrower domains, or different endpoint conventions, the prose uses those exact
conditions.

That rule explains several choices made above:

\begin{itemize}
\item The finite probability approximation uses atomic convolution measures and the exact
      weak-convergence API, not an unformalized continuous box-convolution density.
\item Arbitrary norm-summable vector weights have formal probability, head--tail,
      reflection, continuous-linear naturality, and operator-affine recursion laws;
      their laws have topological support exactly the series range, and arbitrary signed real
      weights have exact min/max interval ranges and supports.  A nontrivial norm-summable
      real-weight law is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure and has null
      singletons.  The
      pushforward-law definition itself is total even without measurability; its
      probability and map identities state the summability, completeness, and Borel
      hypotheses explicitly.  Scalar pointwise naturality alone needs neither summability
      nor completeness.  The generic affine recurrence is conditional on a displayed
      shift relation.  Separately, \path{GeometricUniformLaw.lean} defines the named weights
      \((1-q)q^n\), their series and probability law, and proves self-similarity, reflection,
      absolute continuity, null singletons, and support equal to the series range under
      \(|q|<1\), with exact support \([0,1]\) under \(0\le q<1\).
      \path{ContinuousCDF.lean} and \path{GeometricUniformCDF.lean} add the named CDF,
      continuity and reflection for \(|q|<1\), the exterior tails for \(0\le q<1\), and,
      for \(0<q<1\), the conditioning equations, everywhere derivative, explicit
      compactly supported Radon--Nikodym density, and real-space \(C^\infty\) package.
      \path{ProbabilityRepresentation.lean} supplies the exact half-base CDF and density
      bridges.  \path{AffineIndependentCopy.lean} proves probability preservation,
      product-map and characteristic-function factorization, finite characteristic-function
      iteration, and uniqueness of probability fixed points for \(|q|<1\) in every complete
      second-countable real inner-product target.  Its
      \path{GeometricUniformUniqueness.lean} specialization characterizes the named law,
      including \(q=0\) and negative \(q\), without support, density, or moment assumptions.
      The selected density has no proved interpretation here at \(q=0\) or
      negative \(q\).  \path{GeometricSincFactorization.lean} gives the exact finite
      residual sinc factorization and pointwise phase-bearing prefix limit for every
      \(|q|<1\), with dyadic wrappers; \lean{geometricSincProduct} already packages the
      same rescaled sinc factors as a \lean{tprod}.  A named
      characteristic-function-to-\lean{geometricSincProduct} bridge (or explicit
      \lean{HasProd} theorem), compact-uniform convergence, general-\(q\) rapid decay and
      Fourier inversion, centered packaging, closed
      higher-derivative and endpoint-flatness wrappers, shape results, further transforms,
      and explicit Bernoulli-cumulant/Bell-moment formulas and asymptotics remain outside
      this interface.  The exact-support oriented transform DDE and adjacent Cauchy-power
      recurrence for every nonzero \(|q|<1\), including negative ratios, are direct theorems
      of \path{GeometricUniformCauchy.lean}; these are distinct from the still-open
      characteristic-function-to-infinite-product and Fourier-inversion formulas.
\item Quantile transport is formal at the measure level first for arbitrary real
      probability laws, with no support or range restriction and an almost-everywhere
      sublevel characterization; a compact pointwise specialization feeds the clamped
      Fabius inverse.  This proves strongly measurable
      Banach-valued substitution with Lean's totalized Bochner integral.  A separate
      weighted-subgraph development proves Banach-valued survival layer cake and the
      full-interval Fabius inverse identity for an explicit interval-integrable primitive
      or a pointwise-\(C^1\) primitive.  Its strict-order hypothesis actually makes the
      two compact-interval clock restrictions a Galois connection, so the generic
      explicit and pointwise-\(C^1\) forms now derive clock measurability rather than
      assuming it.  For a real-valued absolutely continuous primitive, a further generic
      subtraction theorem and its normalized and Fabius forms accept every real or complex
      \(L^1\) weight and require no separate complementary-integrability hypothesis.  Finally,
      \path{InversePairIntegral.lean} proves the two-function identity for arbitrary
      absolutely-continuous outer functions on an ordered compact rectangle, derives
      monotonicity on that rectangle and measurable canonical clamped extensions from the
      strict inverse-order relation, and supplies both the Fabius wrapper and its area sum.
      These declarations do not package reversed-endpoint, variable-upper-endpoint,
      higher-order, improper-integral, fractional, complex-valued arbitrary-AC-primitive,
      or complex-Mellin identities.
\item Step cells use the formal half-endpoint indicator, including its point value at a
      shared boundary.
\item The Fourier chapter distinguishes the real punctured-limit statement from the stronger
      proved local theory: every nonzero signed integer has an exact complex analytic order,
      all lower complex derivatives vanish, and the first surviving derivative is evaluated
      explicitly in \Cref{sec:fourier-integer-zero-jets}.
\item Summable-scale infinite products are formal under the exact norm-summability,
      continuity, local-compactness, completeness, and big-\(O\) hypotheses stated in
      \Cref{sec:geometric-reciprocal-gamma}.  For complex \(q\) with
      \(\lVert q\rVert<1\), this gives the entire reciprocal-Gamma product, its zero orbit,
      Mahler and reflection equations, and the dyadic zero and meromorphic pole orders.
      The Gamma-side functions are totalized pointwise inverses: raw products of Gamma
      factors away from their poles, arithmetic canonical regroupings, logarithmic,
      digamma, Malmsten, heat-trace, integral, D-finite, reciprocal-Gamma derivative-tower,
      and general-base order statements are not claimed.
\item Flatness uses natural powers, and the analytic-locus claims use precisely the domains
      proved in \path{NowhereAnalytic.lean}.
\item The \(q\)-calculus distinguishes the half-base Fabius identities from the
      base-generic finite Gaussian/Richardson algebra: recursive Gaussian coefficients,
      functoriality, both Pascal orientations, reflection symmetry, product
      concatenation, the full denominator-free factorial identity, principal
      specializations, denominator-free geometric residual moments, forward closed
      finite-node numerators, and the proved quarter-base specialization are formal.
      Generic finite formal-series filters act diagonally coefficientwise, and exact
      geometric rows have the all-degree Gaussian residual multiplier.  Their analytic
      companion interchanges finite filters with unconditionally summable weighted
      coefficient series, splits exact heads from infinite tails, and gives the exact
      Gaussian \(\mathsf{tsum}\) tail for geometric and Lagrange rows.  Zero-weight nodes
      impose no convergence condition.  Conditionally-only convergent boundary series,
      formal-to-analytic power-series evaluation, a sinc-product instantiation, radii or
      uniform convergence, norm/sign/error bounds, asymptotic estimates, and quarter-sinc
      acceleration remain outside the formal corpus.
\item Integer-order Volterra integration is formal at arbitrary base point and endpoint in
      a real Banach space, including strict FTC, order composition, polynomial weights,
      Taylor reconstruction, and the exact Fabius ladder.  A separate total real-order
      operator over arbitrary real normed spaces has the classical continuous-input kernel
      and additivity theorems when \(0<\alpha\) and \(a\le x\), and agrees with the integer
      operator at every positive natural order.  The shifted real beta kernel is evaluated
      exactly when both orders are positive and the endpoint inequality is strict; its raw
      kernel is interval-integrable also at a coincident endpoint.  In a complete target,
      shifted powers and constants have exact Gamma-quotient values, and two positive
      orders compose for a continuous input on an ordered compact interval.  The
      atom-preserving lower-CDF layer cake also gives the positive-real Fabius stopped-power
      identity for every real endpoint and its unit-endpoint moment.
      Positive affine covariance holds at arbitrary real order for an ordered endpoint and
      positive scale, and Gamma-normalized order raising needs only a continuous primitive,
      an interval-integrable displayed derivative, and that derivative from the right on
      the open interval.  The causal Rvachev primitive, its compact-support cutoff, its
      positive-natural bridge, and its positive-order semigroup are formalized on
      \(x\ge-1\).  The first exact fractional shifts are proved for the signed
      extension on \(x\ge0\), the bounded function on \([0,1]\), and the \(\Up\)-to-\(F\)
      bridge based at \(-1\) on \(x\ge-1\).  The modules prove no corresponding semigroup
      law for merely interval-integrable inputs or noncomplete targets, no Caputo or
      Riemann--Liouville derivative operator, no complex-order result, and no iterated or
      negative-branch shift, shifted lattice, or endpoint-\(\Up\) wrapper.  Separately, the
      weighted inverse-layer-cake API permits real or complex scalar weights and a
      Banach-valued explicit or pointwise-\(C^1\) primitive on the full unit interval; its
      arbitrary-absolutely-continuous form retains a real-valued primitive.  Variable or
      reversed endpoints, complex-valued arbitrary-AC primitives, higher inverse primitives,
      fractional inverse identities, improper-integral limits, and complex Mellin continuation
      remain outside these results.
\item Endpoint asymptotics retain exact phases and the proved remainder scopes; numerical
      fits and unproved coefficient formulas are absent.
\end{itemize}

The source drafts have been discharged into two destinations.  Proved material was rewritten
into the theorem-backed narrative here.  The remaining research material is preserved under

\begin{quote}
\path{Analysis/FabiusFunction/docs/semi-formalized-research-frontiers/}.
\end{quote}

In particular, inverse-function repeated integration and saddle expansions, improper
inverse integrals, complex-power and complex-order integration, conditionally convergent
boundary transforms, sinc-tail instantiations and estimates, small-argument analysis, and
other deferred results remain available there with an explicit warning that they are not
part of the formal corpus.  Positive-real order raising, the first fractional
Fabius--Rvachev shifts, and unconditional generic-\(q\) series transforms have moved into
the theorem-backed narrative.  Natural-order Volterra integration and finite
polynomial weights are formalized in \Cref{sec:normalized-volterra}; the compact-rectangle
absolutely-continuous inverse calculus is \Cref{thm:ac-inverse-pair}; and the finite
base-generic Gaussian/Richardson algebra and its formal and unconditionally summable
series filters are formalized in
\Cref{prop:geometric-principal-residuals,prop:finite-power-series-filter,prop:analytic-series-filter}.

\section{Conclusion}
\label{sec:conclusion}

The formal development links three global presentations without confusing their domains.
The bounded \(F\) supplies the probability and arithmetic viewpoints; the compactly
supported \(\Up\) supplies refinement, Fourier, spline, and Legendre viewpoints; and the
signed \(\mathcal F\) supplies the global dilation and binary-translation formulas.  Exact
bridges connect them to finite atomic laws, weak convergence, centered splines, moments,
generic weighted distributions, geometrically weighted uniform laws, exact quantile
transport, Banach-valued survival layer cake, weighted inverse layer cake,
arbitrary absolutely-continuous inverse-pair calculus, reciprocal-power values, dyadic
algorithms, half-base \(q\)-binomial identities, prefix sums, denominator-free
  arbitrary-base geometric interpolation, finite formal-series and unconditionally summable
  analytic filters with their Gaussian residuals, normalized integer-order and positive-real
  fractional Volterra foundations, exact integer Fabius primitives and first fractional
  Fabius--Rvachev shifts, polynomial weights, endpoint Laplace analysis, and
computable approximation.

The most important methodological conclusion is equally concrete: the primary document is
now a map of proved mathematics.  Research claims can move back from the frontier only after
a matching Lean theorem exists, with the same hypotheses, domain, normalization, endpoint
convention, and conclusion.

\section{Affine Cauchy--Stieltjes calculus}
\label{sec:cauchy-calculus}

The current library packages the report-oriented kernel without tying it to a particular
probability law.  For a finite real measure \(\mu\), write
\[
 \mathcal C_\mu(z)=\int_{\R}\frac{\dd\mu(x)}{z-x},
 \qquad
 \mathcal P_{\mu,n}(z)=\int_{\R}(z-x)^{-n}\dd\mu(x).
\]
These are \lean{measureCauchyTransform} and \lean{measureCauchyPower}, on the natural open
domain \lean{measureCauchyDomain}.  Their integral, index-one, affine-pushforward,
open-domain, derivative, and holomorphy declarations are, respectively,
\lean{measureCauchyTransform_apply}, \lean{measureCauchyPower_one},
\lean{measureCauchyPower_map_affine}, \lean{measureCauchyTransform_map_affine},
\lean{isOpen_measureCauchyDomain}, \lean{hasDerivAt_measureCauchyTransform}, and
\lean{analyticOn_measureCauchyTransform}.  The affine identities are total under Lean's
Bochner-integral convention and allow every nonzero real scale, including a negative one.

The reusable fixed-point interface assumes only that \(\mu\) is finite, its support lies in
a carrier \(K\), the map \((u,x)\mapsto c+au+bx\) preserves \(K\), both \(a\) and \(b\)
are nonzero, and
\[
 \mu=\bigl(\operatorname{Unif}[0,1]\times\mu\bigr)_*
       (u,x\mapsto c+au+bx).
\]
It requires no probability normalization and no closedness, compactness, measurability, or
nonemptiness assumption on \(K\).  Off the complexification of that carrier, the direct
named theorems \lean{hasDerivAt_measureCauchyTransform_of_uniformAffineFixedPoint} and
\lean{measureCauchyPower_succ_of_uniformAffineFixedPoint} give
\[
 \mathcal C_\mu'(z)=\frac{1}{ab}
 \left(\mathcal C_\mu\!\left(\frac{z-c}{b}\right)
      -\mathcal C_\mu\!\left(\frac{z-c-a}{b}\right)\right)
\]
and, for \(n\ge1\),
\[
 \mathcal P_{\mu,n+1}(z)=\frac{1}{nab^n}
 \left(\mathcal P_{\mu,n}\!\left(\frac{z-c-a}{b}\right)
      -\mathcal P_{\mu,n}\!\left(\frac{z-c}{b}\right)\right).
\]

For the geometric uniform law the definitions
\lean{geometricUniformStieltjesDomain}, \lean{geometricUniformStieltjesTransform}, and
\lean{geometricUniformStieltjesPower} are total in real \(q\).  Their remaining direct
public declarations are \lean{geometricUniformStieltjesTransform_apply},
\lean{geometricUniformStieltjesPower_one},
\lean{isOpen_geometricUniformStieltjesDomain},
\lean{analyticOn_geometricUniformStieltjesTransform},
\lean{hasDerivAt_geometricUniformStieltjesTransform_refinement}, and
\lean{geometricUniformStieltjesPower_succ}.  Holomorphy assumes \(|q|<1\); the divided
differential and adjacent-power equations also assume \(q\ne0\).  Their proofs use the
exact series range as invariant carrier, so negative ratios are included.  The case \(q=0\)
is excluded only from the divided formulas.

At \(q=1/2\), for every bounded Fabius solution, the canonical unit and centered
hierarchies are related by the direct named all-order bridge
\[
 S_n(z)=2^nR_n(2z-1).
\]
\begin{center}
\footnotesize\lean{fabiusStieltjesPower_eq_two_pow_mul_rvachevCauchyPower}
\end{center}
The module \path{CauchyTransform.lean} contains six public definitions:
\lean{rvachevCauchyDomain}, \lean{fabiusStieltjesDomain},
\lean{rvachevCauchyTransform}, \lean{fabiusStieltjesTransform},
\lean{rvachevCauchyPower}, and \lean{fabiusStieltjesPower}.  Its direct theorem surface is
\lean{rvachevCauchyTransform_apply}, \lean{fabiusStieltjesTransform_apply},
\lean{rvachevCauchyPower_one}, \lean{fabiusStieltjesPower_one},
\lean{rvachevCauchyTransform_eq_integral_rvachevUp},
\lean{isOpen_rvachevCauchyDomain}, \lean{isOpen_fabiusStieltjesDomain},
\lean{hasDerivAt_rvachevCauchyTransform},
\lean{hasDerivAt_fabiusStieltjesTransform},
\lean{analyticOn_rvachevCauchyTransform},
\lean{analyticOn_fabiusStieltjesTransform},
\lean{fabiusStieltjesTransform_eq_two_mul_rvachevCauchyTransform},
\lean{rvachevCauchyTransform_eq_inv_two_mul_fabiusStieltjesTransform}, the all-order bridge
above, \lean{hasDerivAt_fabiusStieltjesTransform_refinement},
\lean{fabiusStieltjesPower_succ}, and \lean{rvachevCauchyPower_succ}.
In particular, the unit-interval equation
\(S'(z)=4(S(2z)-S(2z-1))\) and both adjacent-order recurrences on their complex slit
domains are now direct named results, not merely informal consequences of affine transport.

This closure does not supply a complex logarithmic fixed point, complex order,
pointwise or nontangential Sokhotski--Plemelj limits, a principal-value Hilbert-transform
identity, or the full Jacobi/Pad\'e theory.  Separate current modules do prove the real
logarithmic identity for \(z>1\), real order lowering for \(\alpha>1\), exterior Laurent
series, finite-height Herglotz--Poisson identities, integrated interval
Stieltjes--Perron inversion, and initial exact Jacobi data; none of those statements is read
as one of the stronger boundary or continuation claims just listed.

\(V_af\) & \lean{volterraPrimitive}\hspace{0.35em}\lean{a}\hspace{0.35em}\lean{f} & first oriented primitive at base \(a\)\\
\(L_a^nf\) & \lean{iteratedPrimitive}\hspace{0.35em}\lean{n}\hspace{0.35em}\lean{a}\hspace{0.35em}\lean{f} & literal \(n\)-fold primitive\\
\(J_a^nf\) & \lean{normalizedVolterra}\hspace{0.35em}\lean{n}\hspace{0.35em}\lean{a}\hspace{0.35em}\lean{f} & normalized Cauchy-kernel primitive\\
\(I_{a+}^{\alpha}f\) & \lean{fractionalVolterra} & total real-order Gamma-normalized Volterra operator\\
\(I_{a+}^{\alpha+\beta}f=I_{a+}^{\alpha}I_{a+}^{\beta}f\) & \lean{fractionalVolterra_add} & positive orders, ordered endpoint, continuous Banach-valued input\\
\(U_\beta(x)\) & \lean{rvachevFractionalPrimitive} & causal Rvachev fractional primitive based at \(-1\)\\
\(I_{0+}^{\alpha}F(x)=\E[(x-X)_+^\alpha]/\Gamma(\alpha+1)\) & \lean{ProbabilityRepresentation.fractionalVolterra_fabiusReal_eq_uniformProduct_integral_posPart} & positive real order, every real endpoint\\
affine \(I_{a+}^{\alpha}\) & \lean{fractionalVolterra_affine} & arbitrary real order, positive scale, ordered endpoint\\
\(I_{a+}^{\alpha+1}g'\) & \lean{fractionalVolterra_add_one_deriv} & positive order, continuous primitive, integrable interior right derivative, exact Gamma boundary term\\
fractional \(\mathcal F\) & \lean{fractionalVolterra_add_one_extendedFabius_of_nonneg} & \(I_{0+}^{\alpha+1}\mathcal F(x)=2^\alpha I_{0+}^{\alpha}\mathcal F(x/2)\), \(\alpha>0\), \(x\ge0\)\\
\(\mathcal A_{S,x,w}(a;z)\) & \lean{finiteAnalyticSeriesFilter} & finite filter of unconditionally summable weighted coefficient series\\
\(I\) & \lean{fabiusInv}\hspace{0.35em}\lean{fabius}\hspace{0.35em}\lean{fabius_spec} & totalized inverse\\
\(c_n\) & \lean{moment}\hspace{0.35em}\lean{n} & even moment\\
\(d_n\) & \lean{halfMoment}\hspace{0.35em}\lean{n} & half moment\\
\(P_w(n)\) & \lean{inclusivePrefixSum}\hspace{0.35em}\lean{w}\hspace{0.35em}\lean{n} & inclusive weighted prefix\\
\(W_{b,w}(n)\) & \lean{weightedScaleMultiplicity}\hspace{0.35em}\lean{b}\hspace{0.35em}\lean{w}\hspace{0.35em}\lean{n} & weighted scale multiplicity\\
\(\nu_b(n)\) & \lean{padicValNat}\hspace{0.35em}\lean{b}\hspace{0.35em}\lean{n} & maximal whole-base power exponent\\
\(\varepsilon_n\) & \lean{thueMorseSign}\hspace{0.35em}\lean{n} & signed Thue--Morse value\\
\(\wt(n)\) & \lean{binaryWeight}\hspace{0.35em}\lean{n} & binary digit sum\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
